Tabular Foundation Models for Credit Risk Prediction: A
Dedicated Review and Empirical Comparison

Medina-Olivares5, Christophe Mues6, María Óskarsdóttir7,8, Seppe vanden Broucke9,1, Tony Van Gestel10, Tim
Verdonck11,12, and Wouter Verbeke1

Abstract
Predictivemodelsarecentraltocreditriskmanagement,wheretheaccuracyofdefault-probabilityand
loss estimates affects the profitability of lending and the stability of the financial system. Decades of
benchmarkingresearchhaveconsolidatedthestateoftheart,withgradientboosting(GB)modelsoften
representingthestrongestmachinelearning(ML)benchmark, whilelinearandlogisticmodelsremain
important regulatory and industry reference models. The recent advent of tabular data foundation
models (TFMs) introduces a new paradigm for predictive modeling. Rather than fitting a model to a
target dataset, they use in-context learning (ICL) to integrate information from the target dataset with
prior knowledge acquired through large-scale pretraining on diverse collections of (synthetic) tabular
data. We conjecture that access to information beyond the target data is beneficial in small-data
settings and may help address longstanding challenges, including low-default portfolios. Whether this
promise holds in practice remains an open question. This paper benchmarks state-of-the-art TFMs
againstabroadsetofclassicalandadvancedMLcompetitorsontwocoretasks: probabilityofdefault
(PD) and loss given default (LGD) modeling. We evaluate33 classification methods across14 PD
datasetsand 27regressionmethodsacross 7LGDdatasets,spanningarangeofperformanceindicators
and experimental conditions. The most recent TFMs, in particular TabPFN-3 and TabICLv2, achieve
the strongest average results, with the largest gains in small-sample and strongly imbalanced settings.
These results are notable because these models are used with their pretrained parameters fixed and
without downstream hyperparameter optimization (HPO) or task-specific parameter updates.
Keywords:Analytics•Credit Risk•Tabular Foundation Models•Probability of Default•Loss Given Default

Tabular Foundation Models for Credit Risk Prediction
1 Introduction
Foundation models are large-scale ML systems trained on vast and heterogeneous corpora, enabling transfer across a
wide range of downstream tasks, often after task-specific fine-tuning (Schneider et al., 2024). They have shown great
promise for natural language processing (NLP) and computer vision, where systems such as GPT-4, CLIP, and diffusion
models advanced text generation, zero-shot classification, and image synthesis (Radford et al., 2021; Bubeck et al.,
2023; Awais et al., 2025), marking a shift from task-specific models toward general-purpose systems.
Tabulardata,thetypicaldataformatincreditriskanalytics,differsmarkedlyfromunstructureddata,duetoheterogeneous
featuretypes,disparatescales,anddataset-specificsemantics. Thesecharacteristicshavehistoricallyimpededknowledge
transfer. Tree-based ensembles, especially gradient boosting machines (GBMs), have long served as the strongest
benchmarks for many tabular prediction tasks, consistently outperforming many task-specific deep learning (DL)
alternatives in broad comparative studies (Lessmann et al., 2015; Gunnarsson et al., 2021; Shwartz-Ziv and Armon,
2022; Grinsztajn et al., 2022). However, recent progress in prior-data fitted networks (PFNs) challenges the status quo
by introducing foundation-model-style prediction for tabular data. PFNs pretrain on vast amounts of (synthetic) data
and transfer the extracted statistical regularities to downstream tasks through ICL (Hollmann et al., 2025; Qu et al.,
2025; Zhang et al., 2025; Ma et al., 2025). At inference, a PFN conditions on labeled examples and outputs predictive
distributions for the unlabeled instances, with no parameter updates or hyperparameter tuning. Specifically, standard
PFNs ingest the entire labeled and unlabeled table in a single forward pass, whereas retrieval-based variants condition
each prediction on a query-specific set of neighbors (Ma et al., 2025).
This paper examines whether this PFN-based TFM paradigm benefits credit risk modeling. Credit risk management is
critically dependent on accurate predictions of risk parameters, notably the PD and the LGD. More accurate credit
decisions directly affect capital requirements, allocation, and portfolio steering under the Basel framework, provisioning
under International Financial Reporting Standards 9 (IFRS 9), and risk-adjusted pricing. These stakes make PFNs
particularly relevant for credit risk modeling practice, because their properties address several operational challenges
faced in credit risk prediction. First, as zero-shot predictors, they avoid repeated hyperparameter tuning and retraining
acrosstasksandportfolios. Thispropertyisattractivefornewordevelopingportfolioswithlimiteddataavailability,such
aslow-defaultportfolios,specializedcorporatesegments,ornewcreditproducts. Second,PFNscouldenableaconsistent
modeling approach across risk parameters (PD, LGD, and potentially exposure at default (EAD)), thereby benefiting
governance by simplifying validation, monitoring, and model risk management. From a supervisory perspective,
cross-parameter consistency within financial institutions (FIs), as well as methodological uniformity across them, may
reduce the burden of model review for these institutions. Third, by combining broad synthetic pretraining with ICL on
institutional data, PFNs may improve sample efficiency and reduce over-reliance on historically biased local patterns,
thereby supporting fairer and more inclusive decision making (Oskarsdóttir et al., 2019; Kozodoi et al., 2022; Moldovan,
2023).
Despite this potential, caution is warranted. Credit risk modeling poses specific challenges (small datasets, severe
target class imbalance, heterogeneous borrower populations, economic cycles, and regulatory constraints) that current
PFN-based TFMs may not fully address (Baesens et al., 2016). Moreover, a systematic evaluation of PFNs for credit
risk prediction has been lacking: while anecdotal evidence points to strong performance on small datasets, it remains
unclear whether PFNs can consistently match tuned baselines across diverse credit portfolios and targets.
We address this gap through three research questions (RQs), each examined through a corresponding empirical analysis.
RQ 1askswhetherPFN-basedTFMsarepredictivelycompetitiveinPDclassificationandLGDregression. Toanswerit,
webenchmarkthesemodelsagainstanextensivesetofclassicalandadvancedMLmethods: 33classificationalgorithms
across 14PD datasets and27regression algorithms across7LGD datasets that vary in size, dimensionality, and target
characteristics. Our evaluation follows the benchmarking tradition in credit scoring, uses cross-validation, and reports a
comprehensive set of performance measures. As a by-product, the benchmark provides the first credit-risk assessment,
to the best of our knowledge, of several recent non-PFN DL models for tabular data (Ye et al., 2024). RQ2asks how the
models perform under data scarcity; we address it through an auxiliary learning-curve experiment that progressively
varies the available sample size. RQ3 asks how robust the models are to class imbalance; we address it through a
second auxiliary experiment that progressively reduces the minority-class proportion in the PD datasets. Together, these
analyses assess both the models’ general predictive competitiveness and how their relative performance changes under
controlled data scarcity and artificial class imbalance.
Theremainderofthispaperisorganizedasfollows. Section2reviewscreditriskmodelingandrecentadvancesinDLfor
tabular data. Section 3 introduces the Bayesian foundations, architectures, and pretraining designs of PFNs. Section 4
formulatesthethreeRQsanddescribesthedatasets,benchmarkedmethods,andevaluationprotocols. Section5presents
the empirical findings and answers each RQ. Section 6 discusses the implications for credit risk management and
research directions, and Section 7 concludes the paper. Appendices A–F provide, respectively, the statistical analysis,
additional performance measures, supporting visualizations, per-dataset prediction distributions, complete rank-based
results, and further dataset documentation, including target distributions, eligibility for the auxiliary experiments, and
the pretraining-overlap audit.
2

Tabular Foundation Models for Credit Risk Prediction
2 Literature review
This paper presents extensive empirical results on the predictive performance of PFN-based TFMs for PD and LGD
modeling. We build on a large body of prior work on credit risk prediction and extend it by introducing a new family of
predictive methods. Beyond credit risk and, more generally, financial applications, the paper also draws on prior work
on DL for tabular data, which paved the way for the recently introduced PFNs. This section reviews prior work in these
two fields and elaborates on our contributions.
2.1 Credit risk modeling
Creditriskreferstothepotentiallossarisingfromacounterparty’sfailuretomeetcontractualobligations(Baesensetal.,
2016). In retail and wholesale banking, risk models quantify key risk parameters used for pricing, capital, provisioning,
and portfolio management: the PD, which estimates the likelihood of a borrower defaulting over a fixed horizon, and
the LGD, which measures the proportion of the exposure that is not recovered if default occurs. In both PD and LGD
modeling, predictive accuracy is key to the efficiency and effectiveness of credit risk management (Baesens et al., 2003;
Loterman et al., 2012; Gunnarsson et al., 2021). Better predictions directly translate into improved capital efficiency
(e.g., when estimating capital requirements under the Basel Accord’s internal ratings-based (IRB) approach), more
precise and timely provisioning under IFRS 9 through improved estimation of lifetime expected credit losses, reduced
impairments, and better pricing decisions (Bastos and Matos, 2022). Regulators likewise emphasize the discriminatory
power and calibration of scorecards, for example, through backtesting, monitoring stability, and validation requirements.
Aiming to unlock financial rewards from more accurate predictions, the community routinely assesses the potential of
new prediction methods and proposes methodological adjustments tailored to characteristic modeling challenges. For
PD, these challenges include, e.g., class imbalance (Brown and Mues, 2012; Marqués et al., 2013; Engelmann and
Lessmann,2021;Wangetal.,2025),rejectinference(BanasikandCrook,2007;Kozodoietal.,2025),oreconomiccycle
dependency (Bellotti and Crook, 2012; Dirick et al., 2019; Djeundje et al., 2025; Distaso et al., 2025). Compared with
PD prediction, fewer studies have considered LGD modeling (Yao et al., 2015; Hurlin et al., 2018; Cheng et al., 2025),
whichmaybeduetothelimitedpublicavailabilityofLGDdatasets. DatasetsfrequentlyusedinLGDmodelingresearch
include theLendingClubandFreddie Macdatasets (Zhou et al., 2018; Calabrese and Zanin, 2022), which provide
sufficient information to approximate LGD. Given the characteristic bimodal shape of real-world LGD distributions,
arising, for example, from mixed portfolios comprising both secured and unsecured loans, many studies recommend
multi-stage models (Tomarchio and Punzo, 2019; Papouskova and Hajek, 2019; Starosta, 2021). Dynamic models
that explicitly recognize capital inflows and outflows throughout the workout period (Thomas et al., 2016) are another
popular research topic in the LGD literature, often involving survival modeling (e.g., Joubert et al., 2021).
Given the prevalence of ML and advanced predictive methods in the credit scoring literature, several benchmarking
studies have periodically consolidated the state of the art by applying an arsenal of statistical and ML methods across
multipledatasets, performancemetrics, andexperimentalconditions. ProminentexamplesincludeBaesensetal.(2003);
Lessmann et al. (2015); Gunnarsson et al. (2021); Jiang et al. (2023) and Loterman et al. (2012); Bellotti et al. (2021),
for PD and LGD prediction, respectively. Closely connected to comparing the predictive performance of alternative
methods is the question of how to properly assess predictions. Several attempts have been made to connect the accuracy
of model predictions to profitability and other measures of business impact (Verbraken et al., 2014; Garrido et al., 2018;
Martin et al., 2025). While quantification of business impact is more developed in PD modeling, some studies have
pursued similar goals in the context of LGD (Hurlin et al., 2018).
Conditional on the availability of suitable indicators of a risk model’s business impact, the predictive models themselves
can be optimized for cost-minimization (Bahnsen et al., 2015) or profit maximization (Finlay, 2010; Serrano-Cinca and
Gutiérrez-Nieto, 2016; Alfonso-Sánchez et al., 2024; Xu et al., 2025). Beyond the actual risk model, other stages in the
modeling pipeline can be optimized for cost/profit objectives. For example, Maldonado et al. (2017); Kozodoi et al.
(2019) introduce techniques for profit-oriented feature selection. Also targeting the input data of predictive models,
recent work aims to extract information from borrower networks that standard methods relying on the independent and
identically distributed (IID) assumption would fail to capture. Using methods from geospatial econometrics, related
studieshave,forexample,establishedcorrelationsbetweendefaulteventsacrossgeographicalneighborhoods(Calabrese
andCrook,2020;Medina-Olivaresetal.,2025). Moregenerally, graph-basedalgorithms, suchasgraphneuralnetworks
(GNNs), facilitate the processing of comprehensive dependency structures, providing modelers with considerable
freedom in how to design an influence network.
Oskarsdóttir and Bravo (2021) pioneered the use of multilayer network analysis in credit-risk modeling, showing that
borrowerconnectionscanprovidesubstantialpredictivegains. Thislineofworkhassincedevelopedtowardgraph-based
and GNN approaches for credit-risk prediction (Shi et al., 2024; Zandi et al., 2025). The pursuit to incorporate auxiliary
information in risk models beyond what classical scorecards embody has also inspired scholars to systematically explore
3

Tabular Foundation Models for Credit Risk Prediction
novel sources of data, including social media postings (De Cnudde et al., 2018), psychometric data (Djeundje et al.,
2021), and textual data (Stevenson et al., 2021; Kriebel and Stitz, 2022; Wu et al., 2025). A distinctive advantage of
such alternative data is that it may facilitate accurate risk assessment ofthin-book clientswhose limited credit history
may otherwise exclude them from accessing financial services (Oskarsdóttir et al., 2019).
Facingincreasingconcernrelatedtotheomnipresenceofadvanced,artificialintelligence(AI)-based,decisionmodelsin
lending (and beyond), much recent work concentrates on Explainable Artificial Intelligence (XAI) techniques to ensure
that the mechanisms underlying such models are well-understood (De Bock et al., 2024; Martens et al., 2026). Model
explainability is a regulatory imperative and prerequisite for achieving higher-level objectives, including robustness,
safety, and fairness (Barredo Arrieta et al., 2020). Recent work on XAI for credit risk examines the interplay between
ML-based scorecards and XAI tools in general (e.g., Bastos and Matos, 2022), the robustness of explanations (Ballegeer
et al., 2025), or the moderating effect of longstanding challenges, such as class imbalance (Chen et al., 2024).
Benefiting from its strong theoretical foundations in cooperative game theory, the SHapley Additive exPlanations
(SHAP) framework for additive feature attribution has gained considerable popularity in the industry (du Toit et al.,
2023; Borgonovo et al., 2024). Unlike SHAP, which decomposes a model’s prediction, be it right or wrong, Hué et al.
(2025) introduces an interesting challenger approach that inherits the same theoretical underpinnings but decomposes a
scorecard’s predictive performance. The corresponding insights might be even more meaningful to decision-makers
as, on the one hand, performance statistics are easier to interpret than predictions, and, on the other hand, knowing
which features drive performance provides directly actionable insights to improve the model. Apart from techniques to
explain otherwise opaque ML models, several studies devise new, intrinsically interpretable learning algorithms and
demonstrate that these often avoid sacrificing (much) predictive accuracy. Representative examples include tree-based
approaches (De Caigny et al., 2018; Carrizosa et al., 2025), generalized additive models (Kraus et al., 2024), and
interpretable DL techniques (Medina-Olivares et al., 2024; Zografopoulos et al., 2025).
Regulatory frameworks governing the use of AI in consumer-facing applications, such as the European Union (EU) AI
Act, the EU General Data Protection Regulation (GDPR), and recent revisions to the Basel capital accord, require FIs to
demonstrate that their risk models are not only explainable but also free from discriminatory bias against historically
disadvantaged groups. Seminal papers provide evidence that scorecards exhibit such biases and that ML-based
approaches are especially vulnerable (Fu et al., 2021; Fuster et al., 2022). Focusing on retail lending decisions, Kozodoi
et al. (2022) analyze available statistical indicators of algorithmic fairness and algorithms to mitigate disparate impact
in the ML pipeline for their suitability in credit scoring. Hurlin et al. (2025) introduce a powerful tool to test a given
scorecard for algorithmic bias, enabling lenders to verify compliance (or the lack thereof) with regulatory requirements.
In summary, ML and AI play a pivotal role in credit risk modeling. They continuously supply novel technologies that
aid lenders in their quest for more effective, efficient, and ultimately profitable decision models that also comply with
regulations.
This paper contributes to the empirical credit risk modeling literature. Although we focus on established classification
andregressionsettingsforPDandLGDmodeling,respectively,ourgoalextendsbeyondassessingyetanotherprediction
method. Instead, we assess anovel paradigmfor risk prediction: the use of tabular data foundation models for zero-shot
prediction. Adopting a zero-shot prediction method to support lending operations entails a fundamental shift in
established risk modeling practices, moving from task-specific model training and calibration to the use of pretrained
foundation models and ICL. Given these consequences and the prevalence of predictive modeling in credit risk, we
consider a holistic evaluation of the new paradigm in canonical risk modeling tasks imperative.
2.2 Deep learning for tabular data
The study follows prior work and established industry practices of approaching PD and LGD modeling as classification
andregressionproblems,respectively. Consequently,weusesupervisedMLalgorithmsforlabeledtabulardata(Baesens
et al., 2016).
DL has revolutionized various data modalities and represents the uncontested state-of-the-art in NLP and computer
vision, to name only a few (LeCun et al., 2015). Given that natural language is a specific type of sequential data, it is not
surprising that DL has also become ago-to methodfor time series forecasting (Benidis et al., 2022). With respect to
cross-sectional tabular data, however, classic ML methods, particularly GBMs, may be considered the de facto standard.
Benchmarking experiments have shown their superiority over DL-based approaches in that the former often provide
more accurate predictions at lower computational costs and/or with less need for hyperparameter tuning (Shwartz-Ziv
and Armon, 2022; Grinsztajn et al., 2022). Shmuel et al. (2025) contextualize this finding by elaborating on the specific
conditions (e.g., dataset characteristics) when DL excels. Considering PD modeling, however, Gunnarsson et al. (2021)
argue that the potential of DL lies in unlocking non-standard data sources (e.g., text) to augment risk models rather than
serving as a vehicle for model estimation.
4

Tabular Foundation Models for Credit Risk Prediction
We argue that the Gunnarsson et al. (2021) study represents the credit risk modeling space well, in particular for tabular
data. DL has provided excellent results in use cases involving semi-structured and unstructured data modalities (Bravo
et al., 2026), such as text data or networks (e.g., Stevenson et al., 2021; Zandi et al., 2025), and settings that employ
time-varying covariates (e.g., Korangi et al., 2023; Medina-Olivares et al., 2024), whereas GBM-type algorithms, often
paired with a post-hoc XAI approach, remain the standard solution when the goal is to develop highly accurate risk
scorecards.1
The unmatched success of DL in unstructured data settings stands in sharp contrast to its moderate performance on
tabular data, which has inspired much recent work aimed at closing the performance gap with GBM-type approaches.
Given that attention-based transformer networks were instrumental to the success of DL in unstructured data settings,
unlocking the power of attention for tabular data was a natural next step.
The work by Gorishniy et al. (2021), introducing FT-Transformer (FTT), may be seen as a seminal paper paving the
way for more advanced DL-based tabular data models. The authors demonstrate effective ways to utilize attention
when working with tabular data, which is essential because it enables models to flexibly capture heterogeneous feature
interactions common in tabular domains. Unlike convolutional or recurrent layers, attention can dynamically weight
dependencies across diverse variables, reducing the need for manual feature engineering. Building on FTT, subsequent
work has refined attention variants for tabular data, confirming its role as a central design principle in closing the
performance gap with GBMs (Ye et al., 2024).
Jiang et al. (2026) provide a comprehensive survey of DL models for tabular data and their evolution. The authors
identify three main evolutionary stages of deep tabular learning. The first stage includes approaches similar to FTT,
whicharetrainedandevaluatedwithinasingledistributionandfocusonfeature-levelencoding,sample-levelinteractions,
and objective-driven regularization (e.g., Huang et al., 2020; Arik and Pfister, 2021). These approaches introduced
innovations such as feature tokenization and attention-based feature selection, but their generalization remained limited
to the dataset at hand.
The second stage, transferable models, decouples representation learning from the downstream task, pretraining a
networkbeforefine-tuningitforprediction. Onebranchreliesonself-supervisedobjectivesthatimprovelabelefficiency
but stay within a single table’s feature space (e.g., Yoon et al., 2020; Ucar et al., 2021; Somepalli et al., 2021). A second
branch confronts the feature- and label-space heterogeneity that blocks transfer across tables: TabLLM serializes each
row into text, namely feature names, values, and a task description, recasting prediction as language generation and
enabling large language models (LLMs) in a few-shot mode (Hegselmann et al., 2023).
The most recent, third stage is marked by foundation models for tabular data, which provide zero-shot predictive
capabilities across diverse domains without the need for task-specific fine-tuning. Within this category, PFNs stand out
asapioneeringapproachthatharnesses(synthetic)pretrainingandICLwithtransformerstodeliverrobustgeneralization
across a wide range of tabular problems (Hollmann et al., 2023; Qu et al., 2025; Ma et al., 2025; Zhang et al., 2025). A
definingarchitecturaldistinctionseparatestheseTFMsfromtheirlanguage-modelcounterparts: whereasstate-of-the-art
LLMs rely on autoregressivedecodersto generate tokens sequentially under a causal mask, TFMs utilize transformer
encodersthat attend bidirectionally across the entire set of rows in a single pass. This design mirrors the structure of
tabulardata, inwhichrowscarrynoinherentsequentialorder. Toachievetheirzero-shotcapabilities, PFNsapproximate
Bayesian inference by pretraining on a large corpus of datasets, which are often synthetic and generated from structural
causal models (SCMs), though in some cases derived from real-world data (Ma et al., 2025), thereby endowing the
transformer with a learned prior that enables strong ICL capabilities. Introducing TabPFNv2, the landmark paper by
Hollmann et al. (2025) demonstrates the generality, predictive capability, and scalability of a PFN-based zero-shot
predictor. The authors conduct comprehensive empirical comparisons to demonstrate that their approach outperforms
GBM algorithms across a large set of regression and classification datasets with up to10,000 samples. However,
because ICL requires the transformer to process the entire dataset simultaneously, TabPFNv2 was computationally
constrained to moderately sized tables (10,000 samples). This scalability bottleneck has driven rapid subsequent
innovations, culminating in architectures like TabPFN-3 and TabICLv2, which extend the operational capacity to
1,000,000observationsonasinglegraphicsprocessingunit(GPU)(Grinsztajnetal.,2026b;Quetal.,2026). Foundation
models for financial prediction are also beginning to extend beyond static tabular data. PRAGMA, for example, learns
reusable representations from raw, multi-source banking event histories and reports strong relative improvements on
internaltasksincludingcreditscoringandfrauddetection(Ostroukhovetal.,2026). Thisdevelopmentiscomplementary
to the PFN paradigm considered here: PRAGMA targets longitudinal behavioral records, whereas our study evaluates
zero-shot foundation models for canonical static PD and LGD tables.
1We do not mean to imply that GBM-type approaches, or any ML-based approach, can be considered an industry standard. Linear
models are the only approach that can potentially claim this role.
5

Tabular Foundation Models for Credit Risk Prediction
The three stages illustrate how the field of deep tabular learning has shifted from approaches that adopt the classic
ML paradigm of training task-specific models to increasingly versatile architectures, which mimic the generality of
contemporary AI models. It is this generality, achieved through ICL and pretraining on large data collections, which we
consider aparadigm shiftwith potentially far-reaching implications for credit risk modeling. This prospect motivates
the focal study, in which we undertake a comprehensive empirical evaluation of PFNs for credit risk modeling.
We acknowledge that our work does not advance deep tabular data learning methodology. Instead, we aim to contribute
original empirical insights into how that field’s latest innovations compare with established domain standards in
credit risk modeling. Such evidence is valuable because the evaluation of PFNs has not moved beyond the standard
benchmarking datasets routinely used in ML. These dataset collections are comprehensive and aim to be general
by combining data from various fields. While clearly effective for demonstrating the potential of a new approach,
decision-makers may appreciate targeted results that reflect the characteristics of their domain. Thus, our study provides
a critical test of whether PFNs can move from promising benchmarks to delivering real impact in credit risk modeling.
6

Tabular Foundation Models for Credit Risk Prediction
3 Prior-data fitted networks
This section introduces the conceptual background of PFNs. We first summarize their Bayesian intuition: a single
pretrained model learns to make predictions from context examples by using regularities encoded in a pretraining prior.
WethendiscussTabPFNasthefirsttabularinstantiationofthisideaandreviewthearchitecturaldevelopmentsthatmade
later PFNs applicable to larger and more heterogeneous datasets. Finally, we explain how synthetic, real, and hybrid
pretraining shape what these models can transfer to PD and LGD modeling, before summarizing the main variants in
Table 1.
3.1 Theoretical foundations
PFNsarepretrainedmodelsthatapproximatetheBayesianposteriorpredictivedistributionatinferencetime,aparadigm
first established in“Transformers Can Do Bayesian Inference”by Müller et al. (2022). The central idea is to train one
transformer, denoted by𝑞𝜃, not on one dataset, but across many supervised learning tasks sampled from a prior. In this
sense, the problem is framed as meta-learning across datasets rather than learning from individual observations within a
single dataset.
The prior encodes assumptions about plausible input–output relationships through a probability distribution𝑝(𝜙) over
data-generating mechanisms𝜙, where each𝜙 determines how features𝑥 arise and how targets𝑦 depend on them.
Synthetic pretraining tasks are generated by first sampling a mechanism,𝜙∼𝑝(𝜙) , and then sampling a dataset from it,
D∼𝑝(D|𝜙).
For each sampled dataset, part of the data is used as labeled context and part is held out as query examples. Let
Dcontext ={(𝑥 𝑖,𝑦𝑖)}𝑛
𝑖=1
denote the context set, and let𝑥∗ denote a query point whose target𝑦∗ is hidden. Under the pretraining prior𝑝(𝜙), the
Bayesian object of interest is the posterior predictive distribution
𝑝(𝑦∗|𝑥∗,D context),
that is, the distribution of plausible target values𝑦∗ conditional on𝑥∗ and on the observed context setDcontext. During
pretraining, the network𝑞𝜃 repeatedly observes such context–query splits and learns to predict the hidden query targets
from the context examples. After pretraining, the same learned predictor𝑞𝜃 can be applied to a new dataset in a
single forward pass, without task-specific parameter updates. In this sense, PFNs amortize Bayesian inference: the
costly learning of how to infer from data is shifted to the offline pretraining phase. In the limit of infinitely many
sampled pretraining tasks, sufficient model capacity, and successful optimization, the learned predictor would recover
the Bayes-optimal posterior predictive distribution implied by the prior (Müller et al., 2022). In practice, performance
depends on finite model capacity, optimization, and the match between the pretraining prior and the empirical structure
of the downstream data.
7

Tabular Foundation Models for Credit Risk Prediction
3.2 TabPFN: the tabular instantiation
Whereas Müller et al. (2022) established the general principle that a transformer can approximate Bayesian inference
from tasks sampled from a prior, Hollmann et al. (2023) instantiated this idea for small tabular classification problems
by designing a tabular pretraining prior and introducing TabPFN, the first tabular instantiation of the PFN paradigm.
Inthenotationintroducedabove,TabPFNspecifiestheprior 𝑝(𝜙) asamixtureoverSCMsandBayesianneuralnetworks
(BNNs), which serve as synthetic tabular data-generating mechanisms. Each sampled mechanism generates a synthetic
datasetD∼𝑝(D|𝜙) . Across millions of such datasets, TabPFN learns to infer the relationship between features and
targets from labeled context examples and to predict the labels of held-out query examples (Hollmann et al., 2023).
BecauseaPFNapproximatestheposteriorpredictivedistributionofitspretrainingprior,boththeBayesianoptimalityof
its predictions and the calibration that accompanies it hold only for tasks whose data-generating structure is adequately
capturedbythatprior(Mülleretal.,2022;Hollmannetal.,2023). Inreal-worldsettingssuchascreditriskmodeling,any
systematic discrepancy between the synthetic pretraining assumptions and actual credit-risk data-generating processes,
known as prior misspecification, can therefore degrade empirical calibration and accuracy on real data. A central lever
for downstream performance is thus not only the model architecture but also the structural realism and diversity of the
data-generating prior. This concern is acute in credit risk modeling, where borrower populations, default processes,
recovery dynamics, and the bimodal loss distributions typical of LGD modeling may differ substantially from the
synthetic mechanisms used during pretraining. Whether a synthetic tabular prior transfers to PD and LGD modeling is
therefore an empirical question, which motivates our domain-specific benchmark.
This prior dependence motivates empirical evaluation, but it does not diminish the practical appeal of the paradigm, for
which two properties are salient. First, TabPFN performs ICL: it reuses the pretrained network to predict the labels for
query observations of a new dataset in a single forward pass, without task-specific gradient training or hyperparameter
tuning. Second, it can be applied to tabular datasets with varying numbers of observations and features, allowing the
same model class to be used across heterogeneous credit portfolios. This flexibility is relevant for lenders, whose
datasets often differ substantially in size, dimensionality, class balance, and distributional characteristics.
3.3 Architectural developments
The original TabPFN encodes each row, that is, a feature vector together with its label, as a single token and applies
attention across the full set of context and query examples (Hollmann et al., 2023). This delivers predictions for a new
table in one forward pass, but makes the cost of attention grow quadratically in the number of rows. Scaling ICL to the
larger and more heterogeneous tables found in real settings therefore became a central architectural challenge, which the
literature has tackled with three broad strategies.
The first strategy keeps the model conditioned on the entire table but redesigns how attention is applied. In place of one
token per row, TabPFNv2 assigns a representation to each cell and uses two-way attention, with each cell attending
to the other features in its row and then to the same feature across all rows (Hollmann et al., 2025). This adds native
support for regression, categorical features, and missing values, and lifts the supported scale to datasets of up to10,000
rows and500 features. TabPFN-2.5 retains this cell-level design but uses a deeper network and a richer synthetic prior,
reaching up to50,000 rows and2,000 features (Grinsztajn et al., 2026a). Mitra reuses the same two-way attention
design and places its contribution in the prior, showing that its mixture of synthetic priors is model-agnostic, improving
both one-dimensional (1D) row attention and two-dimensional (2D) cell attention (Zhang et al., 2025).
The secondstrategy, introducedbyTabICL,treats eventwo-wayattention astoo costly atscale and compresseseachrow
before performing ICL (Qu et al., 2025). A first stage embeds each row with a column-wise Set transformer followed by
row-wise attention, collapsing the feature dimension into a fixed-length vector, and a second stage runs dataset-wise
ICL over these vectors rather than over raw cells. This raises the limit to500,000 rows. Its successor, TabICLv2, adds
regression and reaches one million rows (Qu et al., 2026). The two lines then converge: TabPFN-3 abandons the
alternatingcell-levelattentionofitspredecessors, whichitlikewisefindstoocostlyasdatasetsgrow, returnstorow-level
ICL, and adopts TabICL’s two-stage row compression to scale to one million rows along a cell-budget frontier that
trades rows against features (Grinsztajn et al., 2026b). The third strategy, taken by TabDPT, retrieves only each query’s
nearest rows as context, so the total dataset size no longer directly determines the attention cost (Ma et al., 2025).
8

Tabular Foundation Models for Credit Risk Prediction
3.4 Pretraining data and prior design
Beyond architectural scalability, a second design axis is the pretraining prior. Because the prior shapes the statistical
patterns a PFN learns to recognize and reuse on new tasks, prior design is arguably at least as important as architecture.
Many subsequent PFN variants therefore focus not only on scaling the model, but also on redesigning the synthetic or
real-data distributions used during pretraining. Most models relied entirely on synthetic data-generating mechanisms.
The original TabPFN sampled from a mixture of SCMs and BNNs (Hollmann et al., 2023), whereas TabPFNv2 used
richer SCMs with more elaborate causal graphs and native generation of categorical features and missing values
(Hollmann et al., 2025). TabICL extended this idea by incorporating tree-based SCMs, using XGBoost as a generating
function to inject tree-based inductive biases (Qu et al., 2025). Mitra further systematized prior design by training on a
curatedmixtureofSCMandtree-basedpriors,includingGB,randomforests(RFs),anddecisiontrees(DTs),toimprove
sample efficiency (Zhang et al., 2025). The common lesson is that no single synthetic generator is likely to cover the
diversity of real tabular tasks, motivating increasingly diverse priors in TabICLv2, TabPFN-2.5, and TabPFN-3.
A complementary line of work introduces real datasets into pretraining. Real-TabPFN starts from synthetic-pretrained
TabPFNv2 and continues pretraining on a curated collection of real OpenML and Kaggle tables, improving off-the-shelf
classification performance without changing the underlying architecture (Garg et al., 2025). TabDPT goes further by
pretraining on real data only, combining self-supervised column masking with retrieval-based context selection (Ma
et al., 2025). These approaches reflect the same underlying motivation: real tables may contain statistical structures
that current synthetic priors fail to capture. For credit risk modeling, this issue is particularly important because the
effectiveness of PFNs depends on the extent to which their pretraining data—whether synthetic, real, or hybrid—reflect
the empirical structure of PD and LGD datasets. Appendix F.3 audits direct overlap between our benchmark datasets
and the documented pretraining corpora of Real-TabPFN and TabDPT.

Tabular Foundation Models for Credit Risk Prediction
4 Experimental design
4.1 Research questions
To systematically assess the viability of PFN-based TFMs as a novel paradigm for credit risk modeling, our empirical
study is guided by three core RQs. These questions are designed to evaluate both the general predictive capabilities of
these models and their robustness under structural constraints typical of financial datasets.
RQ1 Predictive competitiveness:To what extent do these models achieve competitive predictive performance in
core credit risk modeling tasks, namely PD classification and LGD regression?
RQ2 Robustness to data scarcity:How does model performance evolve with dataset size? Specifically, do
pretrained TFMs attain stronger absolute predictive performance than conventional learners at common
small-sample budgets?
RQ3 Robustnesstoclassimbalance:Howrobustarethesemodelstosevereclassimbalance—acommonchallenge
in PD modeling associated with low-default portfolios?
By addressing these questions, the subsequent experiments aim to identify not only whether TFMs are competitive, but
also under which specific portfolio characteristics they are most beneficial.
4.2 Data
We evaluate PFNs for PD and LGD estimation, corresponding to classification and regression tasks, respectively. Our
experiments use both public and proprietary datasets spanning retail and small-business portfolios. Table 2 summarizes
the key characteristics of the PD and LGD datasets, including sample size, dimensionality, minority class proportion,
and data origin.
Our datasets vary substantially in the number of observations, the number of features, and the distribution of the target
variable. For PD, the default rate differs substantially across portfolios, ranging from approximately6.7% to 40.0%
(mean≈22.1% ,median≈22.4% ),reflectingtheheterogeneityofcreditproductsandtheirassociatedrisk,fromsecured
mortgages to unsecured consumer loans and subprime credit.
Figure 1 shows the distribution of the target variable for each LGD dataset. Many datasets exhibit the typical bimodal,
zero-one-inflated distribution, with substantial probability mass at or near total recovery (LGD close to zero) and at
or near total loss (i.e., LGD equal to or close to one). This bimodality of the LGD distribution arises due to, on the
one hand, the high risk of losing the entire outstanding amount in the event of default for unsecured exposures, while
for secured loans, on the other hand, the full outstanding amount at time of default is often recovered. Compared to
previous work of Loterman et al. (2012), our data collection exhibits more heterogeneity in the LGD distribution: some
portfolios are dominated by a single mode, whereas others place comparable mass at both boundaries.
A serious risk when benchmarking foundation models using publicly available data concerns leakage. If the pretraining
corpus of a foundation model included some of our benchmarking datasets, this model would face an unfair advantage
over the other contestants. To assess this risk, we audit the pretraining protocols of the foundation models employed,
and report detailed results in Appendix F.3. The key finding of the analysis is threefold: i) most foundation models were
pretrained exclusively on synthetic data, ii) two models were pretrained partly (Real-TabPFN) or wholly (TabDPT) on
real data and warrant closer inspection, iii) the overlap between the pretraining corpus of the two affected models is
small. The empirical results reported in Section 5.1 further shows that the two affected approaches perform much
worse than many alternative learners, far away from the leading group. Therefore, we conclude that there is no material
risk of leakage impeding the benchmarking results, and refer readers to Appendix F.3 for details.
4.3 Data preprocessing
We use Tabular Analytics and Learning Toolbox (TALENT) (Liu et al., 2025) as the benchmarking backbone for the
experiments. TALENT provides a unified interface, ensuring that heterogeneous learners are compared under consistent
data-handlingconventions. However, weperformdataset-specificcleaningbeforepassingadatasetthroughthecommon
modeling pipeline. This one-off curation step consists of deterministic operations that do not interfere with train/test
splitting: removal of identifier or leakage-prone columns, dataset-specific transformations, and, for LGD, clipping of the
target to the valid unit interval[0,1].2
2Only one of our datasets contains raw LGD values outside the unit interval.

The subsequent fold-wise pipeline applies the same rules across datasets. Within that pipeline, all learned preprocessing
parameters (e.g., feature means) are estimated exclusively from the training partition of each cross-validation fold and
applied to the corresponding validation and test partitions, thereby avoiding data leakage. Near-constant features are
identified from the training partition and removed from all three partitions. Extreme numerical outliers are removed
from the training partition only, while extreme validation and test values are winsorized using training-fold quantiles.
For datasets with more than99 retained features, numerical and integer-encoded categorical features are combined,
mean-imputed, and reduced to at most99principal components, with both imputation and PCA fitted on the training
partition. For the other datasets, we impute numerical missing values with the training-fold median, and assign a
separate level to categorical missing values. We then encode categorical features as integer indices for methods that
handle them natively, or via one-hot encoding for methods that require real-valued inputs. Finally, numerical features
are standardized to zero mean and unit variance, except for the TFMs, which receive the same fold-wise processed
inputs without external standardization and apply their own internal normalization. Where required by a learner’s
architectural constraints, a method-specific cap is applied only to its training context; the validation and test partitions
remain unchanged.
4.4 Benchmarking protocol
To assess RQ1, we compare a diverse panel of competitive learners, ranging from classical statistical methods to
state-of-the-art deep tabular architectures. For PD, we compare33 methods, including TFMs, several classical methods,
particularly tree-based ensembles (XGBoost, LightGBM, CatBoost), and a wide array of DL models, including
transformers(e.g.,FTT,ExcelFormer)andspecializedarchitecturessuchasTabNetandTabM.ForLGD,thebenchmark
comprises 27 methods, including the foundation models that support regression, classical regression baselines, GB
approaches, and advanced DL architectures such as ModernNCA, ResNet, and PTARL. Table 3 summarizes the full set
of methods considered across both tasks.
We evaluate performance on the held-out test partitions from five-fold cross-validation. For tunable learners, we select
hyperparameters independently within each fold using Optuna (Akiba et al., 2019) with20 trials on a validation split
comprising 20% of the training fold. We run TFMs in their default in-context configuration without downstream
hyperparameter tuning.
In the main text, we report one primary metric per task: Area Under the Receiver Operating Characteristic Curve (AUC)
for PD, because it evaluates ranking performance independently of a classification threshold, and𝑅2 for LGD, because
it is scale-normalized. Both are comparable across datasets that differ in size and class balance, and standard in the
credit scoring benchmarking tradition (Lessmann et al., 2015). All remaining performance metrics are reported in
Appendix B. For PD these comprise additional discrimination measures (Gini, the Kolmogorov–Smirnov (KS) statistic,

In addition to conventional performance measures, we assess mean calibration at the portfolio level using the pooled
out-of-fold predictions for each dataset. For PD, we compare the observed default rate with the mean predicted PD; for
LGD, we compare the mean realized LGD with the mean predicted LGD. We report differences as observed minus
predicted. Positive values therefore indicate underprediction, whereas negative values indicate overprediction. When
summarizing results across datasets, each dataset receives equal weight.

Tabular Foundation Models for Credit Risk Prediction
4.5 Auxiliary experiments
A feature of particular interest for credit risk modeling concerns the ability of TFMs to handle data scarcity. Therefore,
we augment the main benchmark with two auxiliary experiments that probehowthe leading models behave when data
regimes change. We reuse the main protocol — five-fold cross-validation with the same train/validation/test splits and
the same metrics — but disable HPO (default settings only) and restrict the pool to the strongest foundation models
together with two reference baselines: CatBoost and a logistic or linear regression model. Crucially, every reduced
dataset is built bynested, fixed-seedsubsampling: at each step, the same rows are kept for all methods, and smaller
samples are strict subsets of larger ones, so the resulting trends reflect the change in data, rather than variation due to
random sampling.
4.5.1 Small-data robustness
To respond to RQ2, the first auxiliary experiment assesses how learner performance evolves with dataset size. We cap
the data to a target sizebeforesplitting and sweep this size downward in fine increments (PD:30,000→100 in steps of
25; LGD:4,600→100 in steps of10). The upper bound doubles as the inclusion threshold: only datasets with at least
that number of observations are included in the experiment (9 for PD, and 4 for LGD), so every performance curve
is defined over the full sweep of selected datasets. Because eligibility is determined by the upper sweep bound, this
experiment evaluates nested subsamples of datasets that are natively large; it does not directly evaluate portfolios that
are intrinsically small.
4.5.2 Class-imbalance robustness (PD only)
In the second auxiliary experiment, aimed at addressing RQ3, we assess how robust TFMs are to class imbalance by
progressively removing minority-class (default) observations. We reduce the positive rate from0.15 to 0.0025 in steps
of 0.0005 (≈300 imbalance levels per dataset and method). We retain only datasets with a minority class proportion
above 0.15 and with at least30,000 observations, so that every dataset can be carried through the full sweep while still
containing minority cases at the lowest imbalance setting. This results in6datasets being included in this experiment.
Note that, for each target rate, the minority class is subsampled across the entire dataset (training, validation, and test
partitions). This design creates class imbalance artificially by removing default observations from otherwise larger
portfolios;itdoesnotreproduceallcharacteristicsofnaturallylow-defaultportfolios. AppendixF.2reportsthecomplete
dataset-eligibility table and explains how the inclusion thresholds determine the experimental samples.
14

Tabular Foundation Models for Credit Risk Prediction
5 Results and discussion
5.1 PD and LGD prediction benchmark
We begin with the headline result of the benchmark. Across both prediction tasks, PD (classification) and LGD
(regression), a TFM achieves the best average performance, and in both cases, it is the same model: TabPFN-3. Recall
that foundation models achieve the reported performance off the shelf: GB and deep-tabular baselines are tuned through
HPO, whereas the foundation models are applied in their default in-context configuration, without gradient-based
training or task-specific tuning. We consider it a notable achievement that an untuned, in-context learner surpasses
carefully tuned boosting ensembles, the de facto standard in credit risk modeling. The improvement is small for PD
(an AUC of0.764 against CatBoost’s0.750) but consistent, and substantial for LGD, where the best foundation model
raises𝑅 2 from0.487to0.537, a relative gain of roughly10%.
Figure 2 reports the average AUC of each classifier across the five folds of all PD datasets. The four highest-ranked
methods are foundation models: TabPFN-3 (0.764), TabICLv2 (0.760), TabPFN-2.5 (0.757), and TabICL (0.753).
The strongest conventional learner is CatBoost (0.750), which the leading foundation models edge out. The absolute
differences at the top of the ranking are small, but consistent, suggesting that TFMs set a new benchmark. However,
performanceamongfoundationmodelsisfarfromuniform. Themostrecentgenerationofmodels(TabPFN-3,TabICLv2,
and TabPFN-2.5) forms the leading group, TabPFNv2 and its continued-pretraining variant Real-TabPFN sit mid-table,
and Mitra and the original TabPFN rank among the weakest learners for default prediction.

The same pattern, in sharper form, holds for LGD. Figure 3 reports the average𝑅2 across LGD datasets. TabPFN-3
again ranks first (𝑅2 =0.537 ), followed by TabPFN-2.5 (0.531) and TabICLv2 (0.530). The three best methods are
foundation models: the same three leading models as for PD modeling. The first conventional learner is CatBoost
(0.487), ranked fourth, followed by TabDPT (0.481) in fifth place. The margin over the boosting baselines is wider
for LGD than for PD. This is notable because LGD modeling is widely regarded as the harder problem, a difficulty
usually attributed to the irregular bimodal shape of the target loss distributions. The within-segment heterogeneity is
also stronger for LGD: while the TabPFN and TabICL line dominates the top of the table, Mitra is the single weakest
method overall (𝑅2 =0.166).
The appendices augment the headline results of Figures 2 and 3. Appendix A provides a full statistical analysis for
our main performance indicators (AUC for PD and𝑅2 for LGD), with results of rank-based and pairwise tests on
the significance of performance differences across all learners and selected champions. Appendix B reports average
(over datasets) results for other performance indicators, including Gini, KS, adjusted AP (APadj)) and the Brier score
for PD, and MSE, RMSE, MAE, and medium/maximum average errors for LGD. Appendix C complements these
aggregate results with per-dataset performance matrices and direct comparisons of TabPFN-3 with CatBoost and the
linear baselines.
Takentogether,theobservedresultspointtoashiftinthestateoftheartincreditriskprediction. DTboostingensembles,
long considered the strongest method, are narrowly surpassed for PD and decisively outperformed for LGD by TFMs,
which attain this performance without task-specific training or tuning. This advantage is most pronounced for LGD,
where the leading foundation models open a wide gap over boosting, and more modest for PD, where the best methods
are separated by small margins. This is not an artifact of the performance metric used. As the additional performance
measures in Appendix B show, the foundation models lead across the board. For PD, they top the ranking not only

in terms of AUC, but also for other discrimination measures, error measures, and calibration measures. Hence, their
advantageextendsfromdiscriminationtooverallprobabilisticaccuracy,althoughtheBrierscoredoesnotrevealwhether
mean PD is systematically under- or overpredicted. The same holds for LGD, where foundation models record the
lowest prediction error (RMSE and MAE) and the highest rank correlation with realized losses (Spearman).
In the following, we further summarize the likelihood that a given methodwinsa dataset–fold observation using the
probability of achieving maximum accuracy (PAMA), computed as the relative frequency with which a learner achieves
the single best performance (in a given metric) across all cross-validation splits and datasets (Fernández-Delgado
et al., 2014). We use AUC for PD and𝑅2 for the LGD part. PAMA provides an intuitive, rank-based summary of
competitiveness that is robust to outlying datasets and makes no assumption about the distribution of performance
differences. Figures 4 and 5 report the PAMA results for the PD and LGD benchmarks, respectively. For PD, two
foundation models separate themselves from the field. TabPFN-3 attains the highest AUC on35.7% of fold-level
observations (25 of 70) and TabICLv2 on30.0% (21 of 70); the best conventional learner, CatBoost, leads on only
5.7% (4 of 70), tied with the original TabPFN. Aggregating over the segment, foundation models achieve the best score
on81.4%of all folds (57of70).

For LGD, PAMA paints a similar picture, although the wins are concentrated among five of the six evaluated foundation
models. TabPFN-3 again leads, attaining the best𝑅2 on 34.3% of folds (12of 35), followed by TabPFN-2.5 at17.1% (6
of 35), TabICLv2 at11.4% (4of 35), and TabPFNv2 and TabDPT at5.7% (2of 35) each. The strongest conventional
learner is ModernNCA, which attains the best𝑅2 on 14.3% of folds (5 of 35); no other conventional learner exceeds a
single fold win. Foundation models collectively achieve the best score on74.3% of all folds (26 of 35). Appendix E
reports the complete average-rank results and the corresponding per-dataset rank matrices for both tasks.
16

Tabular Foundation Models for Credit Risk Prediction
Notably, the results from PAMA analysis also contextualize previous findings concerning average learner performance
in that they show how the large error bars in Figure 2 and Figure 3 stem mainly from performance variations across
datasets rather than performance differences across learners, emphasizing how selected TFMs perform slightly but
consistently better than other, established alternatives. Taken together, the results observed thus far provide strong
evidence that recent TFMs are a powerful prediction tool for credit risk management.

5.1.1 Bias in mean predictions
The headline benchmark evaluates predictive performance primarily through AUC for PD and𝑅2 for LGD, while
Appendix B reports a broader set of discrimination, scoring, and prediction-error measures. These results characterize
how accurately the methods rank observations and predict individual outcomes, but they do not isolate whether
predictions are systematically too low or too high at the portfolio level. Figures 6 and 7 complement these results by
examining the signed bias in dataset-level mean predictions for TabPFN-3, TabICLv2, CatBoost, and logistic or linear
regression. Bias is defined as the observed minus the predicted mean, such that positive values indicate underprediction
and negative values indicate overprediction. The left panels compare the observed and predicted means for each dataset,
while the right panels show the corresponding distributions of signed bias. The more detailed prediction distributions
underlying these summaries are reported in Appendix D.
For PD, logistic regression is nearly unbiased at the aggregate level, with a mean signed bias of+0.08 percentage points.
By contrast, both foundation models exhibit a systematic tendency to underpredict mean default rates. The mean signed
bias is+1.42 percentage points for TabPFN-3 and+0.68 percentage points for TabICLv2. TabPFN-3 underpredicts the
mean default rate in all 14 datasets, while TabICLv2 does so in 13 of 14; the sole exception is Vehicle Loan, for which
TabICLv2 overpredicts by4.38 percentage points. CatBoost shows the opposite aggregate tendency, with a mean signed
bias of−1.27 percentage points, although the direction of its bias varies across datasets. Thus, the strong predictive
performance of the foundation models is accompanied by a clear tendency to understate the dataset-level default rate,
particularly for TabPFN-3.
Figure 6 suggests that decision-makers must select specific use cases for foundation models carefully. A tendency to
underestimate portfolio-level default rates is less relevant for an approval scorecard but a serious concern for an internal
ratingmodelusedforriskmanagement. Takingthislatterperspective,onemightpreferthemoreprudentCatBoostmodel
although it is more biased. On the other hand, standard risk management practices involve a multi-layered architecture
in which prediction model facilitate an ordering obligations based on their estimated default-risk, and estimates of
portfolio-level default rates emerge from a dedicated calibration step (Basel Committee on Banking Supervision, 2017).
More generally, potential distortion in probabilistic forecasts of a learning algorithm can be addressed through post-hoc
recalibration, using established methods, such as, Platt-scaling or isotonic regression (Bequè and Lessmann, 2017).
For LGD, the aggregate directional biases are considerably smaller. The mean signed bias is+0.35 percentage points
for TabPFN-3,+0.17 for TabICLv2,+0.58 for CatBoost, and+0.01 for linear regression, indicating little systematic
under- or overprediction on average. These aggregate values nevertheless conceal dataset-specific biases. The most
pronounced underprediction occurs for PropLGD4, where the signed bias reaches+3.72 percentage points for CatBoost

5.2 Auxiliary experiments
Having established that foundation models are competitive across the board, we next examinewhentheir advantage is
largest. The two auxiliary experiments described in Section 4.5 address this question by varying the data regime along
two axes that are considered to be common challenges in credit risk modeling: the sample size (RQ2) and, for PD, the
severity of class imbalance (RQ3).
5.2.1 Data efficiency and the small-data regime
Figures 8 and 9 report the learning curves for PD and LGD, tracing mean AUC and𝑅2 as the capped dataset size
(before splitting) grows from100 observations to the largest common size of the available datasets (30,000 for PD over
9 datasets, 4,600 for LGD over4 datasets). The curves show an overall upward trend for all methods, but the most
relevant pattern is comparative: the foundation models are strongest in the low-to-moderate data regime, where the
performance gap to the conventional baselines is largest, and this gap narrows as more data become available.
For PD, the smallest sample sizes are noisy, but once a minimal context is available, the foundation models pull ahead.
At 750observations,TabPFN-3andTabICLv2reachAUCvaluesof 0.700and 0.693,comparedwith 0.677forCatBoost
and 0.673 for logistic regression. By30,000 observations, they still lead, with AUC values of0.763 and 0.760, but the
gap to CatBoost (0.754) has narrowed. The pattern is even clearer for LGD. At only130observations, TabPFN-3 and
TabICLv2 already achieve𝑅2 values of0.449 and 0.407, corresponding to78%and 71%of their own best performance
over the sweep, while CatBoost reaches0.325 (59%of its own best) and linear regression0.169 (38%). At the largest
sample size, the foundation models still lead, but the gap is smaller: TabPFN-3 and TabICLv2 reach𝑅2 values of
0.576 and 0.577, compared with0.550 for CatBoost. This indicates that the foundation models achieve a large share of

The reported curves in Figures 8 and 9 provide direct evidence in support of the following answer to RQ2: the
pretraining of foundation models translates into markedly higher sample efficiency, and their advantage is greatest in the
low-to-moderate data regime; this indicates a strong potential for adoption in small portfolios, e.g., for project lending or
newly launched products where conventional learners might struggle more due to data scarcity. The same ordering
appears at full dataset size, where the relative gain of TabPFN-3 over CatBoost declines with the number of rows on
both tasks. This is the complementary view to the subsampling design, which by construction evaluates portfolios that
are natively large; Appendix C reports the association and its limitations.
5.2.2 Robustness to class imbalance
The second auxiliary experiment focuses on PD modeling, particularly low-default portfolios, by incrementally reducing
the default rate from0.15 to 0.0025. Figure 10 reports mean AUC across the six included datasets as a function of the
minority-class proportion. The central finding is that the foundation models retain the highest absolute performance
throughout the sweep, and their advantage over CatBoost becomes larger as defaults become rarer. At a15% default
rate, TabPFN-3 and TabICLv2 reach AUC values of0.783 and 0.778, respectively, compared with0.770 for CatBoost
and 0.716 for logistic regression. At the most extreme0.25% default rate, TabPFN-3 and TabICLv2 remain at0.718
and 0.715, whereas CatBoost falls to0.683 and logistic regression reaches0.689. Thus, the gap between TabPFN-3
and CatBoost increases from roughly0.012 AUC points at the least imbalanced setting to roughly0.035 at the most
imbalanced setting. CatBoost is, in fact, the most imbalance-sensitive method in the panel; for the most extreme
setting, its performance drops below logistic regression (0.689), which is otherwise the weakest method3. Among
the high-performing methods, the foundation models are affected least, with TabPFN-3 retaining about92% of its
least-imbalanced AUC at the0.25% setting, compared with88.7% for CatBoost. This is exactly the behavior one would
3This crossing is based on the unsmoothed sweep values; it is not visually apparent in Figure 10, which shows moving-average
trends.

A second observation concerns the performance of the two foundation models. They are close on AUC throughout,
with TabPFN-3 consistently stronger than TabICLv2, especially in the severely imbalanced band of roughly1% to
5% positives (a delta in AUC of about0.010, against0.004 at the least imbalanced setting and a near tie at the0.25%
extreme). Taken together, the class imbalance experiment motivates the following answer to RQ3: foundation models
maintain the highest absolute AUC under severe class imbalance, with TabPFN-3 strongest throughout. This property is
of particular relevance for modeling low-default portfolios.
