Tabular Foundation Models for Credit Risk Prediction
6 Management implications and research directions
The empirical results demonstrate the potential of TFMs for credit risk prediction, particularly in data-constrained
settings. However, their functioning and use differ fundamentally from conventional credit risk models. This section
complements the quantitative assessment with a qualitative appraisal of the implications for credit risk modeling and
management, and maps out selected directions for research.
6.1 Implications for credit risk management
ThissubsectiondiscussesmanagerialimplicationsoftherapidlyevolvingTFM-paradigmandtheobservedbenchmarking
results. We consider how TFMs may affect the economics of model development, the sources of competitive advantage
among FIs, and the organization and governance of the broader credit risk ecosystem.
6.1.1 Model development and competitive capabilities
Afirstimplicationconcernstheroleofpredictivemodelingcapabilitiesasasourceofcompetitiveadvantage. Historically,
advances in credit risk analytics have often been associated with the adoption of increasingly sophisticated modeling
techniques. FIs that were able to deploy superior predictive models could potentially benefit from improved risk
assessment, more accurate capital allocation, and more efficient lending decisions. FinTechs exemplify this strategy.
Exploiting reduced regulatory requirements as non-bank FIs, many FinTechs leverage advanced AI tools to gain market
share and competitive advantages in the lending business. The results reported here suggest that TFMs represent yet
another step in the evolution of prediction technology. In particular, the strong performance of recent TFM generations
suggeststhatthisclassofmodelsisstillevolvingrapidlyandmaynotyethavereachedtheperformanceplateauobserved
for many mature ML approaches (e.g., GB).
At the same time, the long-term strategic implications of this development may differ from the immediate performance
gains observed in empirical benchmarks. Foundation models are designed to be broadly applicable and reusable
across tasks. If TFMs continue to improve and become widely available, advanced predictive capabilities may become
accessible to a larger number of institutions, gradually commoditizing parts of model development. Competitive
advantage would then derive less from the ability to develop a marginally more accurate predictive model and more
fromthe complementary capabilitiesrequiredto usethatmodel effectively: identifying suitableapplications, integrating
predictions into lending processes, validating performance for the relevant portfolio, and maintaining appropriate
governance and monitoring. For example, explainability and fairness remain essential requirements for responsible
creditriskmodelinganddecisioning. TheuseofaTFMdoesnotremoveorfundamentallyaltertheseobligations,which
remain with the deploying institution. Extending the perimeter of TFMs to reflect corresponding requirements in their
prediction also creates opportunity for research, which we discuss in Section 6.2.
Furthermore, the emergence of TFMs does not diminish the importance of institution-specific data. The predictive
performance of a TFM depends on the quality, relevance, and representativeness of the information supplied for ICL.
Our results demonstrate that TFMs can leverage limited data more effectively than many conventional approaches,
but they do not imply that data quality ceases to matter or that performance transfers uniformly across portfolios. On
the contrary, as predictive technologies become more widely accessible, the ability to generate, curate, and govern
high-quality data may become an even more important source of differentiation among FIs. Recent industrial evidence
points in the same direction: PRAGMA’s reported gains stem from pretraining on proprietary, multi-source banking
transactions and event histories, illustrating that foundation-model capabilities may amplify rather than replace the
strategic value of rich institution-specific data (Ostroukhov et al., 2026).
The relevant economic comparison also extends beyond predictive accuracy and the effort required to fit an individual
model. Depending on how a TFM is provided, institutions may incur licensing fees, substantial inference costs, or
expenses associated with adapting their data and technical infrastructure. Reliance on an external model or application
programminginterfacemayadditionallycreatevendordependence, switchingcosts, anduncertaintyaboutfutureaccess,
pricing, or model updates. Conversely, reusable foundation models may reduce repeated expenditures on method
selection, HPO, and redevelopment across portfolios. Assessing their managerial value therefore requires a lifecycle
perspective that compares acquisition, integration, validation, operation, monitoring, and replacement costs with the
benefits of improved predictions and shorter development cycles.
These considerations suggest that TFMs may change the composition rather than eliminate the importance of modeling
capabilities. Whethertheirdiffusionprimarilybenefitsinstitutionswithleanandhighlyautomatedprocesses,established
lenders combining rich proprietary data with mature governance structures, or specialized technology providers remains
an open question.
22

Tabular Foundation Models for Credit Risk Prediction
6.1.2 Governance and the credit risk ecosystem
Beyond the level of individual institutions, the emergence of TFMs may have broader implications for the organization
and governance of the credit risk ecosystem. Credit risk modeling is characterized by a highly decentralized structure in
which FIs develop, validate, maintain, and periodically redevelop proprietary models, often to address highly similar
prediction problems. Foundation models introduce the possibility of a different organizational logic, where substantial
effort is concentrated on the development, pretraining, and validation of a small number of general-purpose predictive
systems that can subsequently be deployed across a wide range of downstream applications.
Such a development could have important consequences for regulatory supervision and model governance. Current
supervisoryframeworksarelargelybuiltaroundinstitution-specificmodelswhosepropertiesmustbevalidatedseparately
byeachinstitutionandreviewedbyregulators. Theemergenceofwidelyadoptedfoundationmodelsraisesthepossibility
that some evidence concerning their general properties, including robustness, stress behavior, and known failure modes,
could be generated centrally and reused across applications. Common documentation and validation resources could
reduce duplication and promote more consistent assessments. They could not, however, replace institution-specific
validation, because realized performance also depends on the target portfolio, data construction, outcome definitions,
implementation choices, and intended use. Moreover, externally developed models may change through new weights,
software versions, or service updates, requiring governance processes that identify material changes and determine
when renewed validation is necessary.
A related implication concerns the long-standing regulatory challenge of achieving comparability of risk assessments
across FIs. Differences in internally developed models have repeatedly been identified as a source of variation in risk
estimates and risk-weighted assets, leading to several revisions of the Basel framework since its inception. To the
extent that multiple institutions rely on common foundation-model infrastructures, part of this variability could be
reduced. Thisraisestheprospectofafutureecosysteminwhichstandardizationandcomparabilityareimprovedwithout
necessarily sacrificing predictive performance or risk sensitivity. At the same time, common modeling infrastructures
cannot ensure comparable risk estimates when institutions differ in their data, definitions, calibration procedures, or use
of model outputs.
The emergence of TFMs may also influence the roles of other actors in the credit risk ecosystem. Credit bureaus
and specialized service providers, which often hold large volumes of data spanning institutions or countries, have
traditionally created value through data provision, score development, and analytical services. In a foundation-model
paradigm, these actors may increasingly participate in the development, maintenance, certification, and distribution of
pretrained predictive systems. This shift could create new forms of specialization and facilitate access to advanced
modeling capabilities, particularly for smaller institutions. It could also concentrate technical knowledge, data, and
marketpoweramongalimitednumberofproviders,therebyincreasingcommondependenciesandthepotentialsystemic
relevance of shared model weaknesses.
Takentogether,theseconsiderationssuggestthatthesignificanceofTFMsmayultimatelyextendbeyondtheirimmediate
predictive performance. Our results establish that they are a competitive modeling approach for contemporary credit
risk prediction, but their managerial value will depend on the full set of capabilities and costs associated with their
deployment. Their longer-term importance may lie in accelerating the diffusion of advanced predictive technologies
while shifting the principal sources of differentiation toward proprietary data, institutional implementation, validation,
governance, and integration. At the ecosystem level, the same development may enable greater standardization and
reuse while creating new dependencies that institutions, service providers, and supervisors must manage.
6.2 Research directions
This invited review evaluates whether TFMs transfer successfully to credit risk prediction. Accordingly, we do not
attempt to formulate a comprehensive research agenda for credit risk management. Instead, we derive three connected
directions from the paper’s conceptual arguments and empirical findings. First, research should establish the boundary
conditions of the observed performance gains and explain why pretraining is especially beneficial when observations or
defaults are scarce. Second, it should determine how TFMs can be calibrated, validated, and governed as pretrained
systems whose predictions depend jointly on an external prior and institution-specific context data. Third, it should
examine whether the paradigm extends beyond cross-sectional PD and LGD prediction to the dynamic, normative, and
decision-dependent problems encountered in lending. Progress in all three directions requires evaluation infrastructure
that makes the provenance of data and pretrained models transparent.
23

Tabular Foundation Models for Credit Risk Prediction
6.2.1 Why, when, and when not do tabular foundation models work?
Our benchmark establishes that recent TFMs can predict LGD and PD accurately, but do not explain the underlying
mechanisms. Understanding them is scientifically important and practically necessary: without an account of what
knowledgeistransferredandwhenitisuseful,aninstitutionhaslimitedgroundsfordecidingwhetherbenchmarkresults
are externally valid and informative about a new portfolio.
The Bayesian interpretation of PFNs provides one possible explanation. Pretraining exposes the model to tasks drawn
from a prior distribution and teaches it to approximate posterior predictive inference when a new labeled dataset is
suppliedascontext;performanceshouldthereforedependonwhetherthepriorassignssufficientprobabilitytostructures
resembling the target task. A complementary frequentist interpretation views a pretrained PFN as a predictor whose
tuning parameters have been selected across many tasks before it encounters the target dataset (Nagler, 2023). Its
small-sample performance may then reflect a favorable bias–variance trade-off induced by cross-task pre-tuning. More
generally, observed gains could arise from the prior, architectural inductive biases, regularization through pretraining,
context construction, internal preprocessing, retrieval, or ensembling.
Comparisons between complete systems cannot separate these explanations because current TFMs differ along most of
these dimensions simultaneously. Mitra emphasizes mixtures of synthetic priors, Real-TabPFN introduces continued
pretraining on real tables, and TabDPT combines real-data pretraining with retrieval-based inference (Zhang et al.,
2025; Ma et al., 2025; Garg et al., 2025). Full factorial experiments would require repeated pretraining and may be
prohibitively expensive. Therefore, we invite research on selective ablations with computationally cheaper designs:
controlled target tasks with known data-generating mechanisms, smaller proxy models, intermediate checkpoints, and
interventions on the composition, size, and labels of the context set. Such studies could quantify how much a prediction
depends on pretrained regularities rather than local evidence and test whether the mechanisms responsible for sample
efficiency also explain the models’ robustness to rare defaults and their particularly large gains for LGD.
The related question ofwhentransfer works should be framed as compatibility between the pretraining distribution and
the target portfolio. Sample size, dimensionality, and class balance provide useful initial descriptors, but compatibility
may also concern nonlinearities and interactions, target shape, missingness and measurement processes, selection
mechanisms, temporal stability, and the definitions of default and loss. Meta-analyses across heterogeneous portfolios
should examine which observable task characteristics predict the incremental performance of a TFM over a strong
task-specific learner. The objective is not merely to identify settings in which average performance is high, but to
establish empirically testable boundary conditions for the value of pretraining.
Further, the research agenda must consider negative transfer. An external prior is valuable when it supplies relevant
information that the target data cannot estimate reliably, but harmful when it favors relationships that are inappropriate
for a particular portfolio. Potential sources of mismatch in credit risk include institution-specific underwriting and
collection policies, selective observation of accepted applicants, changes in borrower populations, economic regime
shifts, and differences in outcome definitions. Research should develop diagnostics that detect when a target task lies
outside the effective support of the pretraining distribution and distinguish prior mismatch from insufficient context or
ordinary estimation error. Such diagnostics should guide an operational response: revising or localizing the context set,
recalibrating or adapting the model, combining it with a locally trained learner, or reverting to task-specific estimation.
Context design is central to this problem because the context set is the application-specific component through which a
PFN learns about a portfolio. Research should examine whether contexts should prioritize recent or locally similar
observations, preserve portfolio-level target frequencies, or be constructed separately for borrower segments and
individual queries. It should also quantify sensitivity to individual context observations, row order, label noise, and
changes in context composition. These questions are especially relevant in low-default portfolios, where a small number
of observations may materially influence both discrimination and probability levels.
Finally, research should determine the appropriate scope of pretraining. General-purpose TFMs seek regularities that
transfer across arbitrary tables, whereas credit-specific models could encode recurring structures across institutions,
products, risk parameters, and economic conditions. Greater domain specificity may improve relevance, but it may also
reproduce historical biases, institutional practices, or definitions that do not transfer. Comparisons between general and
credit-specific priors should establish which aspects of credit-risk structure are sufficiently stable to share. Synthetic
generationoffersonerouteforencodinghypothesizedstructures,whilefederatedorotherprivacy-preservingapproaches
may permit learning from confidential institutional data without pooling raw observations (Naresh and Ayyappa, 2026).
The research target is what information should be transferred and at what level of specificity.
24

Tabular Foundation Models for Credit Risk Prediction
6.2.2 Calibration, validation, and evaluation infrastructure
Strong ranking performance does not by itself make a TFM suitable for credit-risk use. In our benchmark, TabPFN-3
systematically underpredicts average PD, despite leading on discrimination. Future studies should determine whether
such bias arises from the pretraining distribution, context composition, class-prior handling, ensembling, or other
elements of the inference pipeline. They should also assess whether established recalibration methods remain
effective with few defaults and stable across time, borrower segments, and economic conditions. Related work should
investigate predictive uncertainty, including uncertainty arising from limited context, sensitivity to context selection,
and incompatibility between the pretrained prior and the target portfolio.
The foundation-model paradigm also changes the object of validation. Conventional validation focuses mainly on a
model estimated from institutional data. For a PFN, evidence must distinguish among the fixed pretrained system, its
institution-specific context and preprocessing, and the resulting application. System-level validation should document
general capabilities, limitations, pretraining provenance, and known failure modes. Application-level validation should
establishthatthetargetportfolioandcontextareappropriate, thatdiscriminationandcalibrationsatisfytherequirements
of the intended use, and that predictions remain stable under plausible changes in data and context. Lifecycle validation
must additionally account for changes to pretrained weights, software, preprocessing, retrieval, and surrounding
ensembles, any of which may alter predictions without conventional redevelopment.
Interpretabilityshouldbeconsideredwithinthisvalidationframework. Therelevantquestionisnotonlywhetherfeature
attributions can be computed, but whether they are stable when the context changes, whether they primarily reflect local
portfolio evidence or relationships inherited from pretraining, and whether identified relationships can be challenged by
validators and domain experts. Similar distinctions apply to robustness and fairness: a general property reported for the
pretrained system does not establish that it holds for a particular institution, population, or decision process.
Thesequestionsrequireevaluationinfrastructuretailoredtopretrainedtabularmodels. Dataprovenance,transformations,
sampling periods, outcome definitions, and possible pretraining exposure must be documented so that transfer can
be distinguished from memorization or leakage. Model artifacts, checkpoints, inference configurations, and context-
construction procedures should be versioned. Benchmarks should include temporal and cross-institutional holdouts,
controlled changes in target prevalence and context composition, and portfolios selected to vary systematically in their
distance from the pretraining distribution. Secure benchmark environments may permit fixed pretrained models to be
evaluated on confidential institutional data without disclosing the underlying portfolios.
Existing data initiatives illustrate both the opportunity and the limitations. For example, theGlobal Credit Datapools
anonymized internal data from member banks and provides long histories for PD, LGD, and EAD modeling under
common definitions and quality controls4. Similarly, the European DataWarehouse5 collects and validates standardized
loan-level information across European securitized and private portfolios. Such infrastructures could support analysis
acrossproducts,institutions,jurisdictions,andeconomicconditionsthatconventionalpublicbenchmarkscannotprovide.
Access restrictions, confidentiality, and differences between recorded and required variables nevertheless imply that
open datasets, controlled-access repositories, institutional collaborations, and confidential benchmark environments will
remain complementary. Their common objective should be a reproducible evaluation of both the pretrained system and
its portfolio-specific use.
6.2.3 Extending the scope of foundation-model credit risk analytics
Once the mechanisms, boundary conditions, and validation requirements of existing TFMs are better understood, a
further question is whether the paradigm can address credit-risk problems beyond static PD classification and LGD
regression. Three extensions concern distinct dimensions of the modeling problem: survival and longitudinal models
change the structure of the predicted outcome; fairness requirements concern properties that predictions and decisions
should satisfy; and causal models change the inferential question from forecasting outcomes to estimating the effects of
interventions. Recent prototypes show that each dimension can, in principle, be represented through pretraining, but
none yet constitutes an established credit-risk solution.
A first direction concerns survival and longitudinal modeling. Default, recovery, prepayment, and completion of the
workoutprocessarenaturallyrepresentedastime-to-eventormultistateprocesses(Baesensetal.,2016;Medina-Olivares
et al., 2025). SurvPFN demonstrates that a PFN can be pretrained on synthetic survival tasks, incorporate censoring,
and produce continuous-time predictive distributions (Böhm et al., 2026). Credit applications, however, involve
competing events, transitions among account states, and evolving inputs such as repayments, transactions, arrears, and
macroeconomic conditions. Research should test whether pretraining improves the estimation of rare transitions and
4https://globalcreditdata.org
5https://eurodw.eu
25

Tabular Foundation Models for Credit Risk Prediction
newly observed portfolios, whether it preserves calibration across economic regimes, and whether a common model can
combine static borrower characteristics with irregular longitudinal histories more effectively than separately developed
task-specific models.
Aseconddirectionconcernsfairness. Lendingdecisionsaffectaccesstofinanceandmaydisadvantageprotectedgroups,
while the meaning of fairness depends on the product, jurisdiction, affected population, and decision context. FairPFN
illustrates that a model can be pretrained on synthetic causal structures to attenuate the influence of a protected attribute
withoutrequiringthetargetdataset’scausalgraphasaninput(Robertsonetal.,2024). Thisdemonstratesmethodological
feasibility, but does not resolve the normative choice among incompatible fairness criteria or guarantee transfer under
causal misspecification. Research should examine multiple and intersecting protected attributes, alternative sources
of direct and indirect discrimination, sensitivity to the assumed causal structures, and stability under population
change. Corresponding work can clarify whether pretrained models can implement institutionally specified constraints
transparently and consistently.
A third direction moves from predicting risk to estimating the consequences of credit decisions. Lenders intervene
through pricing, credit limits, contract terms, restructuring offers, and collection strategies (Fahner, 2012). Selecting
among these actions requires counterfactual evidence rather than predictions under the historically observed lending
process. CausalPFN-style models suggest that treatment-effect estimation can be amortized across simulated data-
generating processes, in a manner related to the use of synthetic causal structures in FairPFN (Balazadeh Meresht et al.,
2025). Yet pretraining cannot make causal identification automatic. Approval policies determine which outcomes are
observed, relevant confounders may be unavailable, and interventions can be continuous, dynamic, and interdependent
(Kozodoi et al., 2025; Bockel-Rickermann et al., 2025; Alfonso-Sánchez et al., 2024). Research must therefore establish
which identification assumptions are encoded in the prior, how they can be communicated and challenged, and whether
the resulting models improve treatment-effect estimation and decision quality in realistic pricing, limit-management,
restructuring, and collection settings.
These extensions should remain connected to the core questions developed above. Their value depends on prior–task
compatibility, their outputs require application-specific calibration and validation, and their evaluation demands
longitudinal, action, and protected-group data that are often unavailable in public benchmarks. The central research
opportunity is therefore not merely to create credit-specific versions of SurvPFN, FairPFN, CausalPFN, or other
instances of PFN-type estimators. It is to determine whether foundation-style pretraining transfers reliably to the
dynamic,normative,anddecision-dependentconditionsofreallending,andtoidentifywhenspecializedorconventional
approaches remain preferable.
26

Tabular Foundation Models for Credit Risk Prediction
7 Conclusions
This invited review examines the emergence of TFMs as an important development in tabular ML and assesses their
relevance to credit risk modeling. We review the conceptual foundations, architectural evolution, and pretraining
strategies of PFN-based models and complement this synthesis with a domain-specific empirical evaluation. The
review explains how TFMs shift part of learning from task-specific model fitting to reusable pretraining and in-context
inference, while the empirical analysis tests whether the resulting capabilities transfer to PD and LGD prediction under
conditions characteristic of credit portfolios.
Regarding predictive competitiveness (RQ1), the results favor the most recent foundation models. TabPFN-3 ranks first
for both tasks, attaining the highest average AUC for PD classification and the highest average𝑅2 for LGD regression.
Foundation models as a group achieve the best score on81.4% of PD folds and74.3% of LGD folds. Their margin
over the strongest GB baseline is narrow for PD but substantial for LGD. However, this result is driven by the latest
TabPFN and TabICL generations rather than by the model class as a whole. Moreover, strong discrimination does not
ensure unbiased probability levels: TabPFN-3 underpredicts the mean default rate across all14PD datasets, whereas
the corresponding LGD biases are small. Consequently, its use for applications requiring probability levels, including
provisioning or capital calculations, requires an appropriate calibration step.
The auxiliary experiments provide initial evidence on how the observed gains vary with common credit risk modeling
characteristics. Regarding sample efficiency (RQ2), TabPFN-3 and TabICLv2 lead in the low-to-moderate data regime
forbothtasks,withtheiradvantageoverCatBoostgenerallynarrowingasmoreobservationsbecomeavailable. Regarding
class imbalance (RQ3), the foundation models retain the highest absolute AUC as defaults become increasingly
rare, while the gap between TabPFN-3 and CatBoost widens. Taken together, these findings indicate that pretraining
is especially valuable when the target portfolio contains limited information, whether because few observations are
available or because the event of interest is rare. This makes recent TFMs particularly relevant to low-default portfolios,
specialized segments, and newly launched products.
The reviewed literature and the empirical evidence suggest that TFMs represent more than another family of tabular
prediction algorithms. By combining regularities acquired across pretraining tasks with evidence supplied through an
institution-specific context, they introduce a distinct modeling paradigm whose performance depends on both sources of
information. Their strong results demonstrate the potential value of this transfer, but variation across model generations
and the observed PD bias also reveal current limits. Understanding compatibility between the pretrained system and
the target portfolio, including the possibility of negative transfer, is therefore central to explaining when these models
should be preferred to conventional task-specific methods.
The broader significance of these findings is that TFMs change where learning takes place and, consequently, how credit
risk modeling must be organized and studied. Their adoption may reduce the importance of repeatedly developing
models from scratch, while increasing the importance of understanding how pretrained knowledge interacts with
institution-specific data and requirements. This perspective connects the managerial and research agendas: both
depend on distinguishing the general capabilities of a pretrained system from its suitability for a particular portfolio.
Recent TFMs should therefore be regarded as serious candidates for contemporary credit risk prediction, particularly in
information-constrained settings, while their wider adoption depends on a better understanding of the mechanisms,
limits, and reliability of transfer.

Tabular Foundation Models for Credit Risk Prediction
Appendices
These appendices provide the analyses and documentation that support the paper but are not reported in full in the
main text. Appendix A presents the statistical analysis, and Appendix B reports performance measures beyond the two
primarymeasures,AUCforPDclassificationand 𝑅2 forLGDregression. AppendixCprovidesadditionalvisualizations
and supporting results, Appendix D shows the per-dataset prediction distributions underlying the mean-bias analysis,
and Appendix E reports the complete rank-based summary. Finally, Appendix F documents the target distributions,
eligibility for the auxiliary experiments, and overlap between the evaluation datasets and the documented pretraining
corpora of models pretrained on real data.
A Statistical analysis
A.1 Statistical methodology
We compare the methods across datasets by combining an established significance-testing protocol with a Bayesian
analysis and a descriptive summary, and we apply the same procedure to PD classification and LGD regression. The
backbone is the protocol of Demsar (2006): a Friedman omnibus test of whether the methods differ at all, followed, only
if it rejects, by pairwise rank comparisons with a correction for multiple testing, reported as a CD diagram and a matrix
of adjusted𝑝-values. Classical significance tests of this kind report only whether a difference is unlikely to have arisen
by chance, not how large or how probable it is, and they lose power when the datasets are few, so we complement them
with the Bayesian signed-rank test, which returns the probability that one method is better or that two methods are
practically equivalent, and with a descriptive Win/Loss matrix. Finally, because the full method panel is large relative to
thenumberofdatasets,wealsoreportanexploratorycomparisonofacompact,data-selectedpaneloffamilychampions.
The omnibus test asks whether the methods differ at all. We use the Friedman test (Friedman, 1937), which ranks the
methods on each dataset and tests whether the average ranks depart from equality, in the less conservative𝐹-distributed
form derived by Iman and Davenport (1980). The average ranks on which these tests operate are reported in full in
Appendix E.
Whentheomnibustestrejects,weexaminepairwisedifferencesusingtwocomplementarypost-hocproceduresappliedto
the same average ranks. The CD diagram, introduced for this purpose by Demsar (2006) on the basis of the all-pairwise
rank test of Nemenyi (1963), places the methods on the rank axis and connects methods that are not separated by the
Nemenyi test. Alongside it, we report a matrix of pairwise𝑝-values adjusted with the step-down Shaffer procedure of
García and Herrera (2008). This procedure can be more powerful than applying one common critical distance to every
pair (García et al., 2010) and is the all-pairwise rank counterpart of the Holm correction (Holm, 1979).
The Bayesian signed-rank test, introduced by Benavoli et al. (2014) and applied here following the tutorial of Benavoli
et al. (2017), is the Bayesian counterpart of the Wilcoxon signed-rank test (Wilcoxon, 1945). For a pair of methods it
returns the posterior probability that one is better, that the other is better, or that the two are practically equivalent, the
last defined by a ROPE of±0.01 in the metric (AUC or𝑅2). Unlike a significance test it can therefore support, and not
only reject, the hypothesis that a difference is too small to matter in practice.
We then report the Win/Loss matrix, which is descriptive rather than inferential: each cell counts the datasets on which
one method beats another, shaded by the net margin, and so conveys the direction and consistency of head-to-head
dominance that a single significance verdict discards.
Thebenchmarkcontainsmanymethods(33forPDclassification,27forLGDregression)butcomparativelyfewdatasets
(14 and 7). On a panel of that size the all-pairwise post-hoc has little power and the CD grows so large that the diagram
cannot separate the leading methods from one another. We therefore repeat the Nemenyi and Shaffer comparisons on
a reduced panel of champions: the single best-performing method of each modeling family on the primary metric,
together with the linear model that remains the standard reference in credit scoring practice. For PD classification
the champions are TabPFN-3 (the best foundation model), CatBoost (the best GB ensemble), T2G-Former (the best
deep tabular network), and logistic regression; for LGD regression they are TabPFN-3, CatBoost, RealMLP (the best
deep tabular network), and linear regression. This reduced panel provides a focused comparison with the strongest
representative of each established family. Because the champions are selected using the same benchmark on which
they are compared, however, this analysis is exploratory and its adjusted𝑝-values do not account for model-selection
uncertainty. The linear model is retained for its role as the industry benchmark rather than for its performance.

Tabular Foundation Models for Credit Risk Prediction
A.2 PD classification
The Friedman test strongly rejects equal performance on the full panel of 33 methods (𝐹𝐹 =10.32 ,𝑝=6.9×10 −36;
𝑁=14 ,𝑘=33 ), providing evidence that performance differs across methods. Localizing the differences on this panel
is, however, uninformative. The Nemenyi CD spans roughly 13.9 rank positions, so the CD diagram merges almost
every method into a single indistinguishable group, and the Shaffer correction flags only 61 of the33
2
 =528 pairs,
essentially all of them separations of the leading methods from the weakest (Naive Bayes, TabNet, KNN, the NCM
classifier) rather than distinctions among the competitive methods at the top. We therefore reserve the CD diagram and
the adjusted𝑝-value matrix for the exploratory champion comparison and characterize the full panel with the Bayesian
test and the Win/Loss matrix.
The Bayesian signed-rank test (ROPE±0.01 AUC) compares TabPFN-3 against its closest rivals. Against TabICLv2,
the next foundation model, it assigns probability 0.76 to practical equivalence and 0.24 to TabPFN-3 being better, with
no posterior mass, at the reported precision, on TabICLv2 being better. Against CatBoost, the strongest GB model, it
assigns probability 0.57 to TabPFN-3 being better and 0.43 to practical equivalence, with no posterior mass, at the
reported precision, on CatBoost being better. Against logistic regression, it assigns probability 0.87 to TabPFN-3 being
better. These comparisons favor TabPFN-3 over the linear baseline, while its difference from CatBoost frequently lies
within the ROPE. The Win/Loss matrix (Figure A.2) shows a similar descriptive pattern: TabPFN-3 and TabICLv2 post
positive net win rates against nearly every competitor.
Within the exploratory four-champion comparison (TabPFN-3, CatBoost, T2G-Former, and logistic regression), the CD
diagram (Figure A.1a) places TabPFN-3 ahead of the other three, which are not separated from one another by the
Nemenyi test. The Shaffer-adjusted matrix (Figure A.1b) reports adjusted𝑝-values below the conventional threshold
for the three comparisons involving TabPFN-3. These reduced-panel results do not account for the data-dependent
selection of the family champions.

A.3 LGD regression
The Friedman test rejects equal performance on the full panel of 27 methods (𝐹𝐹 =8.64 , 𝑝=3.3×10 −19; 𝑁=7 ,
𝑘=27 ). As for PD classification, the full-panel post-hoc is uninformative: the Nemenyi CD spans about 15.7 rank
positionsandtheShaffercorrectionflagsonly24ofthe 27
2
 =351 pairs,againstrong-versus-weakcontrasts(theleading
foundation models against SVM, KNN, TabNet, and linear regression). With only seven datasets, the pairwise tests
have limited power; a nonsignificant adjusted comparison should therefore be interpreted as inconclusive rather than as
evidence of equality.
The Bayesian signed-rank test (ROPE±0.01𝑅 2) provides a complementary probabilistic comparison. For TabPFN-3
against TabICLv2, it assigns probability 0.59 to practical equivalence, 0.38 to TabPFN-3 being better, and 0.03 to
TabICLv2 being better. Against CatBoost, it assigns probability 0.75 to TabPFN-3 being better, 0.20 to practical
equivalence, and 0.05 to CatBoost being better; against linear regression it assigns probability 0.97 to TabPFN-3 being
better, with no posterior mass, at the reported precision, on linear regression being better. Thus, where the adjusted
pairwise test does not separate TabPFN-3 from CatBoost, the Bayesian analysis assigns a high posterior probability
to TabPFN-3 being better. The Win/Loss matrix (Figure A.4) shows a similar descriptive pattern, with the leading
foundation models posting strong head-to-head counts and Mitra the conspicuous exception at the bottom.

Figure A.3: Post-hoc champion-level LGD comparison of the four selected family representatives (TabPFN-3, CatBoost,
RealMLP, linear regression) on𝑅2. (a) Nemenyi CD diagram of the average ranks (1 = best); methods connected by
a bar are not significantly separated. (b) Matrix of Shaffer-adjusted pairwise𝑝-values; a cell is green where the two
selected methods differ significantly and red where they do not. TabPFN-3 is shown in red.
Within the exploratory four-champion comparison (TabPFN-3, CatBoost, RealMLP, and linear regression), the CD
diagram (Figure A.3a) separates TabPFN-3 from linear regression, while RealMLP and CatBoost fall within the CD.
The Shaffer-adjusted matrix (Figure A.3b) reports adjusted𝑝-values below the conventional threshold for TabPFN-3
against RealMLP and linear regression, and for CatBoost against linear regression. The Bayesian analysis additionally
assigns a high posterior probability to TabPFN-3 outperforming CatBoost, although the adjusted frequentist comparison
does not separate them.
Across both tasks, the omnibus tests provide evidence of performance differences, while the full-panel post-hoc
comparisons primarily separate the leading methods from the weakest because the panel is large relative to the number
of datasets. The Bayesian comparisons generally favor TabPFN-3 over the selected conventional baselines, although
practical equivalence retains substantial probability in some comparisons. The reduced champion analysis provides a
focused but exploratory comparison because the family representatives were selected on the same benchmark.

Tabular Foundation Models for Credit Risk Prediction
B Additional performance measures
The main text reports a single primary measure per task (AUC for PD classification and𝑅2 for LGD regression).
A single measure can, however, mask trade-offs, so we report a broader battery here, grouped by the aspect of
performanceitcaptures. Theleadingfoundationmodelsremainamongthestrongestmethodsacrossmostdiscrimination,
probabilistic-scoring, error, fit, and correlation measures, while other methods lead on some threshold-based, robust, or
worst-case measures.
B.1 PD classification measures
ForPDclassificationwegroupthemeasuresintothreefamilies. Thediscriminationmeasures(AUC,theGinicoefficient,
the KS statistic, and adjusted AP) assess how well the predicted scores rank defaulters above non-defaulters and are the
quantities of primary interest in credit scoring. AP summarizes the precision–recall curve as a recall-weighted mean of
precision across score thresholds. Precision–recall measures provide a useful complementary perspective in imbalanced
classification because they explicitly reflect the quality of predictions for the positive class (Saito and Rehmsmeier,
2015). Unlike AUC, AP depends on the positive-class prevalence𝜋: the random-ranking reference value of AP is𝜋.
We therefore report adjusted AP, following the chance adjustment applied to AP by Campos et al. (2016),
APadj = AP−𝜋
1−𝜋 ,
where𝜋 is the fold-specific default rate. The adjustment maps the random-ranking reference to zero and perfect AP to
one, while values below the random reference can be negative. It aligns these reference points across datasets but does
not make AP fully invariant to class prevalence; precision-based metrics remain sensitive to the class prior (Siblini
et al., 2020). The proper scoring rules (the Brier score and log loss) assess overall probabilistic accuracy; they combine
calibration and refinement rather than isolating calibration. The threshold-based measures (accuracy, balanced accuracy,
𝐹1, precision, recall, and the MCC) summarize the confusion matrix at a single operating point; we set that threshold for
each model by maximizing the𝐹1 score on the validation split. Table B.1 reports all of them. TabPFN-3 attains the best
value on every discrimination measure and on both proper scoring rules, and on most threshold-based measures; the
isolated exception, top recall for XGBoost, reflects a different operating point (XGBoost gains recall by predicting more
positives, at a cost in precision). The ordering among the foundation models is the same as on AUC, indicating that the
ranking is not an artifact of the primary measure.

Tabular Foundation Models for Credit Risk Prediction
B.2 LGD regression measures
ForLGDregressionwereporttwofamiliesofmeasures. Theerrorandfitmeasures( 𝑅2, theMSE,theRMSE,theMAE,
the MedAE, and the maximum error) summarize how close the predictions are to the realized losses;𝑅2, the MAE, and
theMedAEarethequantitiesmostcommonlyemphasizedintheLGDliterature. Thecorrelationandexplained-variance
measures (the Pearson and Spearman correlations and the explained variance) summarize how well the predictions track
the realized losses. Table B.2 reports all of them. The three leading foundation models (TabPFN-3, TabPFN-2.5, and
TabICLv2) occupy the top of𝑅2, the RMSE, both correlations, and the explained variance; TabPFN-3 is best on most
of these measures, while TabPFN-2.5 has the lowest MAE. ModernNCA has the lowest MedAE, a robust measure of
typical error, whereas Mitra has the lowest maximum error, a worst-case measure. These exceptions do not alter the
broader pattern across fit, mean-error, and correlation measures.

tunable methods are tuned by HPO on a per-fold validation split; the TFMs are run untuned in their default in-context
configuration. Arrows indicate optimization direction (↓lower is better; all others higher is better). The best value in
each column is shown inbold.

Tabular Foundation Models for Credit Risk Prediction
C Additional visualizations and supporting results
This section collects additional visualizations that complement the main text. We first give a dataset-level view of
performance across the full method panel, then two head-to-head comparisons of the strongest foundation model,
TabPFN-3, against the GB champion (CatBoost) and against the linear industry baseline (logistic regression for PD
classification, linear regression for LGD regression), and finally an analysis of how the relative advantage of TabPFN-3
over the GB champion varies with dataset size.
C.1 Dataset-level performance matrix
The aggregate rankings in the main text average over datasets and can obscure dataset-level variation. Figures C.1
andC.2reporttheper-datasetperformanceofeverymethod,AUCforPDclassificationand 𝑅2 forLGDregression, with
methods ordered best-first and cells shaded from green (best) to red (worst). The matrices confirm that the advantage of
the leading foundation models is broad rather than driven by a few datasets, and they also expose the heterogeneity
discussed in the main text, including the small number of datasets on which the gap to the strongest baselines narrows.

C.2 TabPFN-3 versus the gradient-boosting champion
GB ensembles, and CatBoost in particular, are the strongest conventional benchmark in both tasks. Figures C.3 and C.4
compare TabPFN-3 against CatBoost dataset by dataset, with the diagonal marking equal performance, so that points
above it are datasets on which TabPFN-3 wins. TabPFN-3 attains the higher mean AUC on 13 of the 14 PD datasets and
the higher mean𝑅2 on 6 of the 7 LGD datasets. The advantage is therefore consistent in sign on all but one dataset per
task. Its mean is larger for LGD regression, although the LGD differences vary substantially across datasets.

Tabular Foundation Models for Credit Risk Prediction
C.3 TabPFN-3 versus the linear industry baseline
Linear models—logistic regression for PD classification and linear regression for LGD regression—are included here
not for their predictive performance but because they remain widely used reference models in credit scoring practice
(Lessmann et al., 2015; Dastile et al., 2020). Figures C.5 and C.6 contrast TabPFN-3 with these baselines directly.
TabPFN-3 attains a higher mean AUC than logistic regression on all 14 PD datasets and a higher mean𝑅2 than linear
regression on all 7 LGD datasets, making the descriptive direction of the difference consistent across the datasets in this
benchmark.

Tabular Foundation Models for Credit Risk Prediction
C.4 Does the advantage over gradient boosting grow on small data?
A natural question is whether the advantage of TabPFN-3 over the GB champion is concentrated on smaller datasets, as
earlier foundation-model evidence might suggest (Hollmann et al., 2025; Erickson et al., 2025). Figures C.7 and C.8
plot, for each dataset at its full size, therelativeperformance gain of TabPFN-3 over CatBoost, that is, the improvement
expressed relative to CatBoost’s own score rather than as an absolute difference, against the number of rows on a
logarithmic axis; points above the zero line are datasets on which TabPFN-3 wins. Almost every point lies above
zero: TabPFN-3 attains the higher five-fold mean on 13 of the 14 PD datasets (mean difference+0.0148 AUC, median
+0.0099) and on 6 of the 7 LGD datasets (mean difference+0.0493 in𝑅2, median+0.0261). On both tasks the single
dataset on which CatBoost wins is a large one: Home Credit for PD, with307,511 observations, and PropLGD1 for
LGD, which with58,862observations is the largest LGD dataset in the benchmark.
Both panels show the same descriptive tendency, namely that the relative gain shrinks as datasets grow. The fitted
trend declines by1.30 percentage points per tenfold increase in the number of rows for PD classification and by17.67
percentage points for LGD regression. For PD, the relative gain is+4.5% on HMEQ (5,960 observations) and+4.4%
on Thomas (1,225 observations), against+0.2% on Cobranded (80,000) and+0.4% on GMSC (150,000). For LGD,
the same ordering is far steeper, running from+49.5% on PropLGD5 (594 observations) and+15.8% on PropLGD4
(762) down to+2.6%on LendingClub (5,627) and−2.9%on PropLGD1.
Three qualifications are in order. First, with 14 and 7 heterogeneous datasets respectively, these associations are
descriptive and should not be interpreted causally or as population-level results; the scatter is substantial in both panels,
since the largest PD relative gain,+5.9% on PropPD1, occurs at100,000 observations, and on the LGD side Freddie
Mac attains+9.6% at 16,002 observations, more than two of the five smaller LGD datasets. Second, the two slopes
are not directly comparable in magnitude, because dividing by CatBoost’s own score amplifies differences wherever
that score is low, which is common in LGD regression. The direction of the LGD trend is robust to its most extreme
point but its magnitude is not: excluding PropLGD5 leaves the slope at−7.5 percentage points per tenfold increase.
The PD slope is stable in both respects, moving to−1.5 when its most influential point, PropPD1, is excluded. Third,
dataset size is confounded with the other characteristics that vary across portfolios, including dimensionality and target
distribution. Subject to these qualifications, the patterns on both tasks are consistent with, but do not independently
confirm, the controlled subsampling experiment in the main text (RQ 2), in which sample size is varied within datasets
while all other characteristics are held fixed.

D Per-dataset prediction densities
The dataset-level means in the main text compress each dataset to a single pair of numbers. The figures below show
the full out-of-fold prediction distributions underlying those summaries, with one row per dataset and one column
per compared method. For PD, each panel contains the two class-conditional kernel densities of the predicted default
probability. For LGD, where the target is continuous, each panel contains a 2D kernel density of the raw prediction
against the realized value.

Tabular Foundation Models for Credit Risk Prediction
E Rank-based results
ThemaintextandAppendixBcomparemethodsonthelevelofthemetric, whichisinformativebutsensitivetodatasets
on which all methods score unusually high or low. A rank-based view removes that sensitivity by scoring each method
only against its competitors within a dataset, and it is also the quantity on which the omnibus and post-hoc tests of
Appendix A operate. We therefore report the average rank of every method, together with the per-dataset ranks from
which it is computed.
E.1 PD classification
Figure E.1 reports the average rank of each method on AUC across the 14 PD datasets, and Figure E.2 shows the
underlying per-dataset ranks. The rank view reproduces the ordering of the main text at the top and sharpens the
separation. Thefourbestaverageranksallbelongtofoundationmodels,TabPFN-3( 2.64),TabPFN-2.5( 6.14),TabICLv2
(7.36), and TabICL (7.86), and the best conventional learner, CatBoost (11.00), is a clear distance behind, just after the
best deep tabular network, T2G-Former (10.21). TabPFN-3 is the single best method on 5 of the 14 datasets and never
falls below rank 11, which is the narrowest range of any method in the panel.
TwoobservationsarevisibleintherankviewbutnotintheaverageAUC.First,consistencyseparatesthetworunners-up:
TabPFN-2.5 overtakes TabICLv2 on average rank despite a lower average AUC, because TabICLv2 combines five
outright wins with one dataset on which it falls to rank 29, giving it a rank standard deviation of8.93 against 4.61 for
TabPFN-2.5. Reporting the mean metric alone conceals this difference in cross-dataset rank consistency, which matters
for a practitioner choosing a single model to deploy across a portfolio landscape. Second, one of the weakest methods
on average, the original TabPFN (25.57), attains the top rank on one dataset, confirming that no method in the panel
dominates uniformly and that dataset-level heterogeneity is substantial.

60

Tabular Foundation Models for Credit Risk Prediction
E.2 LGD regression
FigureE.3reportstheaveragerankon 𝑅2 acrossthe7LGDdatasets,andFigureE.4showsthecorrespondingper-dataset
ranks. The separation is sharper than for PD classification. The three leading foundation models occupy the top three
average ranks, TabPFN-3 (2.43), TabICLv2 (2.86), and TabPFN-2.5 (3.71), and none of them ever falls below rank 8.
CatBoost, the best conventional learner, follows at5.71 and TabDPT at6.71. TabPFN-3 and TabICLv2 each take the
top rank on two of the seven datasets, while TabPFN-2.5, TabDPT, and ModernNCA take it once each; TabDPT’s rank
standard deviation of4.98and worst rank of 17 nonetheless make it far less consistent than the leading three.
The rank view also isolates the one clear failure among the foundation models. Mitra has the worst average rank of the
entire 27-method panel (24.57), and the per-dataset matrix shows this is uniform rather than driven by one portfolio: its
best rank on any LGD dataset is 17. The foundation-model family is therefore not homogeneous, and the strong results
of its leading members are carried by the recent TabPFN and TabICL generations rather than by the paradigm as such.

F Dataset details
This section documents the benchmark datasets in more detail than Table 2 allow. Appendix F.1 reports the distribution
of the LGD target. Appendix F.2 records which datasets enter the two auxiliary experiments and why the inclusion
thresholds take the values they do. Appendix F.3 reports an overlap audit between our evaluation datasets and the
pretraining corpora of the two benchmarked models that are pretrained on real data, Real-TabPFN and TabDPT.
F.1 Target distributions
Figure F.1 shows the distribution of the target variable for each LGD dataset. The bimodal, zero-one-inflated shape
described in Section 4.2 is visible in most panels, with substantial probability mass at or near total recovery and at or
near total loss, but its severity varies considerably: some portfolios are dominated by a single mode, whereas others
place comparable mass at both boundaries.

F.2 Dataset eligibility for the auxiliary experiments
Both auxiliary experiments described in Section 4.5 sweep a data characteristic downward from an upper bound, and
that upper bound doubles as an inclusion threshold: a dataset can only be carried through the full sweep if it starts above
the bound. The thresholds were therefore not chosen a priori but read off the data, by taking the largest bound that still
admits a workable number of datasets. For PD, a bound of30,000observations is exactly the size of the smallest dataset
it retains, Taiwan Credit Card, and admits nine of the fourteen PD datasets; raising the bound would drop Taiwan Credit
Card without adding anything, since the next smallest eligible dataset has80,000 observations, and lowering it would
shorten every curve for the sake of datasets that are too small to contribute a meaningful sweep. For LGD, a bound of
4,600observations is set just below the size of PropLGD2, the smallest dataset it retains with4,637observations, and
admits four of the seven LGD datasets; the next candidate has only2,545 observations, so any bound that admitted
a fifth dataset would nearly halve the length of the sweep for the other four. In both cases the bound maximizes the
number of included datasets subject to keeping the sweep long enough to characterize the small-data regime, which is
the object of interest.
Table F.1: Datasets included in the auxiliary experiments. Experiment 2 (sample efficiency) requires at least30,000
observations for PD and4,600 for LGD. Experiment 3 (class imbalance, PD only) additionally requires a minority-class
proportion above0.15.

PD3 GMSC✓

PD5 HMEQ
PD6 Home Credit✓

LGD4 PropLGD2✓n/a

LGD6 PropLGD4 n/a
LGD7 PropLGD5 n/a
The imbalance experiment applies a second criterion on top of the size threshold. Because the sweep starts at a
minority-class proportion of0.15 and only removes minority observations, a dataset must already have a positive rate
above 0.15 to enter at the starting point without oversampling. Six of the nine size-eligible PD datasets satisfy this;
the three that do not are GMSC, Home Credit, and PropPD2, whose default rates lie between6.7% and 9.3% and
are therefore already below the starting point of the sweep. Table F.1 records the resulting inclusion pattern. One
consequence is worth noting for interpretation: because both experiments select on size, they describe the behavior of
the methods on subsampled versions of large portfolios rather than on the natively small datasets in the benchmark.
Likewise,theimbalanceexperimentcreateslowdefaultratesartificiallybyremovingminorityobservationsandtherefore
does not reproduce all characteristics of naturally low-default portfolios. Appendix C.4 provides the complementary
view at full dataset size.
F.3 Overlap with pretraining corpora
Because TFMs are used off the shelf with their pretrained parameters fixed, direct overlap between an evaluation
dataset and a model’s pretraining corpus could lead to an unfair advantage. For models documented as using synthetic
pretraining data only, direct real-dataset overlap is absent by construction. As summarized in Table 1, this applies to
TabPFN, TabPFNv2, TabPFN-2.5, TabPFN-3, TabICL, TabICLv2, and Mitra. This does not rule out indirect benchmark
exposure through model-development or selection decisions. Two of the benchmarked models are pretrained wholly or
partly on real data and therefore require an explicit check. Real-TabPFN continues the pretraining of TabPFNv2 on71
curated tables drawn from OpenML and Kaggle (Garg et al., 2025), and TabDPT is pretrained on123OpenML tables
64

Tabular Foundation Models for Credit Risk Prediction
using self-supervised column masking (Ma et al., 2025). Both papers document a decontamination procedure, but in
each case that procedure was executed against the authors’ own evaluation suites, the OpenML AutoML Benchmark for
Real-TabPFN and CC18 together with CTR23 for TabDPT, so neither provides a guarantee for a downstream benchmark
such as ours.
We therefore compared every dataset in our benchmark against the complete corpus listings published by both models,
matching on dataset name, source repository, target variable, and dimensions, and treating a corpus entry as an overlap
when it is the same dataset or a subsampled or otherwise reduced version of it. Table F.2 reports the outcome. Three
overlaps are identified, all of them on the PD side. Real-TabPFN’s corpus contains the vehicle financing dataset that we
use as Vehicle Loan. TabDPT’s corpus contains a reduced version of GMSC, listed under a generic name but carrying
the same target variable, and a subsampled version of Taiwan Credit Card. No other dataset in the benchmark, public
or proprietary, matches an entry in either corpus. German Credit, in particular, is absent from the TabDPT corpus
because it belongs to CC18, the suite against which TabDPT deduplicated. No LGD dataset overlaps with either corpus:
Real-TabPFN is a classification-only model whose corpus contains no regression tasks, and none of our LGD datasets
appears in the TabDPT listing.
Table F.2: Overlap between the benchmark datasets and the pretraining corpora of the two benchmarked models that
are pretrained on real data. All other benchmarked foundation models are documented as using synthetic pretraining
data only, so direct real-dataset overlap cannot occur under their reported pretraining designs. Only datasets with an
identified overlap are listed; no overlap was found for any other dataset in the benchmark.
ID Dataset name Corpus Corpus entry
PD3 GMSC TabDPT OpenML 44089, reduced to 16,714 rows
PD10 Taiwan Credit Card TabDPT OpenML 45020, subsampled to 13,272 rows
PD12 Vehicle Loan Real-TabPFN Kaggle, full dataset
65

Tabular Foundation Models for Credit Risk Prediction
The available sensitivity checks suggest that these identified overlaps do not drive the aggregate conclusions. First,
neither affected model ranks among the top three on either task, while TabPFN-3, TabICLv2, and TabPFN-2.5, which
occupy the top three positions for both PD and LGD, are documented as using synthetic pretraining data only; TabDPT
places sixth of the33 PD methods on mean AUC and fifth among the nine foundation models. Second, for each
affected model, performance on the overlapping datasets can be compared descriptively with performance elsewhere.
For TabDPT, the mean rank is13.0 over the two overlapping datasets and12.5 over the twelve remaining ones, and
its AUC advantage over the panel mean is smaller on the overlapping datasets (+0.0065) than on the remaining ones
(+0.0157). It places fifth among the nine foundation models on both overlapping datasets, matching its median position
across the full benchmark. The two overlapping datasets are themselves dissimilar: on GMSC, the larger of the two,
TabDPT ranks21st of33methods and falls below CatBoost, whereas on Taiwan Credit Card it ranks fifth. Removing
both datasets from the average moves TabDPT from sixth to seventh place of33, a shift that reflects its near-tie with the
adjacent methods rather than a loss of advantage. For Real-TabPFN, the comparison with its TabPFNv2 backbone is
also informative because continued pretraining includes Vehicle Loan. On Vehicle Loan, Real-TabPFN attains0.6277
against the backbone’s0.6279, a difference of−0.0003 that lies near the center of the differences observed on the
thirteen remaining datasets (mean−0.0002, range−0.0017 to+0.0013). Its rank on Vehicle Loan,26th of33, is worse
than its median rank of17th on the remaining datasets. Third, no LGD dataset is identified in either documented
real-data pretraining corpus, so the entire LGD comparison is free of documented overlap. With two overlapping
datasetsforonemodelandonefortheother, thesecomparisonsaredescriptiveratherthaninferential: wedonotobserve
an advantage associated with the identified overlaps, but cannot rule out an effect. We report them because reuse of
public credit datasets in foundation-model pretraining corpora is likely to become more common as those corpora grow,
and because the same consideration applies whenever a pretrained model is evaluated on data that may have entered a
public repository.
66
